#### Abstracts
##### **Latent Lattice Language Models**
2611.21608v1 by Shivam Duggal, Zongze Wu, William T. Freeman, Antonio Torralba, Richard Zhang, Eli Shechtman

Language models typically increase per-token computation through deeper or looped layers and latent reasoning steps, while richer residual connections improve information flow across layers. We instead increase computational capacity: the number of active computational states a model maintains. We introduce the Lattice of Latents, a set of per-layer horizontal latent streams that run recurrently along time, alongside the vertical residual stream that flows through depth. Like hyper-connections, mHC, and attention or value residuals, the Lattice enhances the residual stream, but rather than adding or rerouting residual pathways through depth, it provides companion computational states that persist across tokens. Unlike hybrid Transformer-SSM architectures, whose recurrent states primarily serve as internal memory for sequence mixing, these latents directly condition the vertical stream's attention, KV-cache writes, MLP computation, and decoding. On Transformers trained on 20B FineWeb-Edu tokens, a 406M-parameter Lattice model lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 to 14.40 over a parameter-matched Transformer. These gains extend across architectural variants. The Lattice composes with prior residual-stream enhancements: with hyper-connections and input residuals, it lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 (base Transformer) and 14.22 (Transformer with the same enhancements) to 13.61. On Gated DeltaNet, Mamba-2, Triadic GDN, and GDN-Mamba-2 hybrids, it improves perplexity at every tested native state size, with Lattice-GDN and Lattice-Triadic-GDN variants outperforming pure baselines with 4x larger native states. These results identify persistent per-layer state as a complementary axis for scaling language-model computation.

摘要：

##### **ViRA: Toward Ultra-High-Resolution Visual Agents via Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization**
2611.21605v1 by Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tongyan Wang, Xuyang Chen, Ruihua Shi, Zhichao Xu, Jiaxin Qin, Guoshenghui Zhao, Baijian Yang, Yingjie Victor Chen, Tan Yu, Weijie Zhao

Visual agents answer questions about ultra-high-resolution photos by zooming into the relevant regions. However, existing agents often answer from common sense instead of reading the evidence. Reinforcement learning (RL) on the final answer cannot correct this, because a guess earns the same reward as reading. We propose Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization (CrossRPO), which trains agents on contrast sets. A contrast set asks one question about several versions of an image that differ only in a small region with the evidence and have different answers, so only reading the evidence solves the whole set. CrossRPO rewards solving the whole set, and we prove that it does not reinforce a set answered from common sense. We build contrast sets for training (ViRA-Train) and evaluation (ViRA-Bench) by editing one small object in images of 21 to 120 megapixels, and we photograph further sets with real changes (ViRA-Bench-Real) to exclude editing traces. We train two Visual Reasoning Agents, ViRA-9B and ViRA-27B, with CrossRPO on only about 3,500 items of ViRA-Train each. CrossRPO achieves 63.3% set accuracy on ViRA-Bench at the maximum resolution, against 59.3% for GRPO. ViRA-9B is the most accurate open model of its size on all benchmarks, and ViRA-27B outperforms Claude Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6-Luna, both at their highest thinking level, on most benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **Quantifying the added value of human forecaster discussions for tropical cyclone intensity prediction**
2611.21602v1 by Ilia Azizi, Frederick Iat-Hin Tam, Marc-Olivier Boldi, Valérie Chavez-Demoulin, Tom Beucler

The National Hurricane Center routinely releases Tropical Cyclone Discussions that offer insights into its tropical cyclone forecasts. This study examines the added value of these discussions for intensity forecasting beyond a set of numerical predictors. For 637 Atlantic and eastern North Pacific tropical cyclones from the 2002-2023 hurricane seasons, we analyze the discussion text and its combination with 13 hurricane environmental predictors from SHIPS and ERA5 10-meter wind fields, for forecasting intensity changes at lead times of 24 to 120 hours using three modeling methods. In one approach, an end-to-end deep learning framework for the text shows that the discussions alone offer comparable value for 24-hour intensity forecasts to the SHIPS predictors. Adding discussion information to the SHIPS predictors in an ensemble of five such models reduces the mean absolute error of 24-hour maximum sustained wind forecasts for 2022-2023 cyclones from 9.89 to 8.90 kt, with a confidence interval that excludes zero when whole storms are resampled. The ERA5 wind fields have a minor effect on mean absolute error in wind predictions, and model rankings differ across predicted variables, lead times and model types. We provide prediction intervals for these forecasts, whose coverage falls below the 90% target during rapid intensification. Analysis of the text shows that words related to intensity and intensity change (such as intensify, weaken, eyewall and category) are important for the model predictions. This retrospective study shows the added value of the forecasters' discussions and suggests that multi-modal learning can help understand the predictive contribution of expert human judgment. The reanalysis data differ from real-time analyses, and the discussions are issued at least three hours after the valid time of the other inputs, so these results should not be interpreted operationally.

##### **How VLMs Plan What's Next**
2611.21600v1 by Rohit Gandikota, Arnab Sen Sharma, Adam Belfki, David Bau

When a vision-language model describes an image, it names one object at a time and it rarely repeats itself, yet how the model plans this order is far from obvious. We characterize the mechanism a VLM uses to decide where to look before it looks. The mechanism has four main parts. First, binding heads match the relevant text tokens against the image and resolve them into pointers to the matched image region. Second, the same binding heads bind each written answer to the region it came from, and the model turns it into a progress signal: a cursor, the position in the image up to which the list has been written. Third, a select step chooses the nearest unwritten region after the cursor, or stops. Finally, gaze heads execute the chosen pointer and return a concept of what is drawn there, which language heads and the late MLPs turn into a word in the language of the question. We validate each part of this mechanism through causal patching in Qwen3-VL models on a synthetic visual retrieval task, and test our mechanisms against alternative hypotheses. We further show that the mechanism holds across input formats, natural photographs, and languages, and that its binding, relay, and gaze heads appear at every model size from 2B to 32B. This work allows us to investigate how a VLM turns a question and an image into a sequence of answers, layer by layer and token by token. More broadly, it opens a way to study how models plan over complex multi-step tasks with long rollouts. Our project is available at https://plan.baulab.info/

Job titles and job descriptions alone provide limited insight into how technical roles differ in a changing job market. We employ GPT-6 Luna to classify job description passages into Team context, Role task and Qualifications. We then create combinations of these sections and examine whether embedding and clustering the segmented passages produce meaningful semantic clusters about different job roles. We use Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) to rank observed differences between embedding distributions and employ a pretrained sparse autoencoder (SAE) to extract features from layer-19 representations of Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Lastly, we employ GPT-6 Luna to come up with hypotheses about job roles' feature patterns, and GPT-6.1 Terra to investigate whether those patterns exist in held-out job descriptions from unseen companies.

##### **When Tool Names Are Not Identities: Measuring Provider Misbinding in MCP-Based AI Agents**
2611.21577v1 by Zeynab Anbiaee, Sajjad Dadkhah, Ali A. Ghorbani

Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly act as agents that select and invoke external tools. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes this interaction by allowing clients to compose tools advertised by multiple servers. However, tool metadata used for discovery and selection is not, by itself, necessarily bound to a verified provider identity. This paper studies this gap as tool-provider misbinding: a failure in which a request intended for a legitimate provider is instead resolved to an attacker-controlled tool. The attacker does not compromise the host, LLM, legitimate server, or network channel; it only operates another MCP server that advertises tools with chosen metadata. We formulate tool provider misbinding as an attacker-controlled tool-selection optimization problem and evaluate it in a controlled MCP environment across five LLM-based clients. The results show that interactions drive misbinding. The strongest failures occur when exact-name collision, task-aligned description, and compatible schema reinforce one another, but authority-enhanced descriptions with compatible schema can also cause wrong-provider execution or unresolved provider ambiguity without name collision. In systematic experiments using three established severity measures on nine regime shifts from three standard traffic datasets, we show that these measures do not reliably predict degradation for instance-normalized TSFMs. The regimes rated most severe are not the regimes that degrade the models most, and degradation reaches 29% in the lowest-scoring regime. ##### **Right Reason, Wrong Advice: Evaluating Recourse in LLM Explanations of Denied Requests**
2611.21545v1 by Arunkumar V

Language models increasingly write the explanations people receive when a rule system denies their request. If several rules deny the request independently, an explanation can name a true reason and still leave the person denied after they fix it. We call the missing property recourse validity: changing what the explanation cites, without changing what was asked for, can flip the decision. We compute it exactly, and a hitting-set argument predicts from the policy alone when a correct reason fails. In six of seven rule systems ported from access control and US benefit eligibility, a single correct reason almost never gives recourse when several reasons apply. Across 20 LLMs, checks of whether the cited reasons are correct, computed or asked of an LLM judge, are close to chance at spotting this failure, as the argument predicts. A strong judge asked whether acting on the explanation would work does spot it. Stronger and reasoning models do better but still fail on many denials with four or more independent reasons. Asking for every rule helps every open model with room to improve. Explanations of eligibility decisions should be built from the decision function and judged by whether acting on them works.

If these shared layers have the same input (i.e., are twins), then we need only perform this shared forward pass once, reducing inference time. However, twin layers are often of varying sizes, and methods from prior work tend to favor the larger layer. In contrast, our approach boosts performance by combining Factor Component Balancing (FCB), which ensures every shared member is well represented in the selected factors, with Block Contribution Balancing (BCB), which considers downstream model effects. Further, we also show that an overparameterized BTWF, where some layer's factors result in higher FLOPs than the original during training, can boost task performance without additional resources at test time. Experiments on six models over nine benchmarks show BTWF obtains a 2-6% gain over the state-of-the-art without expensive finetuning (1-3.5% gain with it) at comparable computational costs.

##### **A Scalable Multi Task Learning Framework for Modeling Demographic Disagreements in Annotation Tasks**
2611.21458v1 by Soumyajit Gupta, Maria De-Arteaga, Matthew Lease

Annotators from different demographic backgrounds often disagree in systematic ways due to varying lived experiences, cultural contexts, and their relationship to the content. When such disagreement occurs, one group's perspective may sometimes warrant prioritization because they hold an epistemically advantaged standpoint or bear greater stake in the outcome. For example, when assessing toxicity of posts on social media, it may be important to prioritize a targeted group's viewpoint. We propose a Stakeholder-Aware Joint Multi-Task Learning (SAJ-MTL) framework to jointly model demographic disagreement both across- and within- annotator groups. The model uses conditional gradient routing to handle sparsity induced by group-conditional labeling, and parameterization supports customized weighting of groups in different task settings. SAJ-MTL improves group-level performance in toxicity detection through better context-aware understanding of demographic-sensitive content. Only annotator group-membership information is required, which is more practical than existing methods requiring more privacy-invasive individual-level annotator details. Our scalable architecture is also computationally efficient to support large-scale deployment.

Question answering over ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing scenes poses a tension for multimodal large language models: global overviews obscure small objects and local attributes, whereas detailed crops consume substantial context. Across two generations of Qwen-VL on LRS-GRO, a 64-token memory achieves 7.6- to 9.9-fold compression of retained crop tokens and improves accuracy by 2.5 to 4.8 percentage points over the downsampled global image alone, recovering most of the high-resolution crop gain with consistent behavior on XLRS-Bench. These findings establish that compact visual latents retain useful local evidence in UHR remote sensing and identify evidence availability as a key factor governing their contribution.

##### **Learning to Reason with Predictive Gains for Generative Recommendation**

However, existing methods primarily optimize reasoning through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with rule-based rewards determined by final recommendation performance, which faces two key limitations: (i) unmeasured predictive contributions within reasoning, where the predictive contributions of individual reasoning steps are not directly reflected in optimization signals, and (ii) weakly discriminative learning signals within groups, where different reasoning trajectories within the group often receive indistinguishable rewards, limiting effective policy optimization. In this paper, we propose GainReason, a simple yet effective training framework that optimizes reasoning through Predictive Gain (PG). We further theoretically establish the alignment between expected PG and the recommendation ranking objective. Building on this signal, we develop PG-based Reasoning Optimization (PGRO) to optimize reasoning generation with sentence-level rewards, supported by a custom causal attention mechanism for efficient PG computation. Additionally, an auxiliary PG-based Reasoning Utilization (PGRU) task adaptively strengthens reasoning-conditioned prediction to complement PGRO. Extensive experiments on three public benchmarks demonstrate that GainReason consistently outperforms competitive baselines and achieves substantial performance gains from reasoning.

##### **A Reinforcement Learning Method to Navigate SMEFT Operator Space**

In particle physics, discrepancies between experimental measurements and Standard Model (SM) predictions are commonly interpreted within new theoretical models. However, exploring the enormous space of possible SMEFT operators rapidly becomes a challenging combinatorial problem: many operators can contribute simultaneously to different observables, their effects are correlated, and distinct operator combinations can provide comparably good descriptions of the data. In this work, we formulate the exploration of the SMEFT operator space as a sequential decision-making problem using reinforcement learning (RL). Our RL agent employs a lightweight actor-critic architecture with approximately 40K trainable parameters in total. Rather than searching for a single best-fit theory, our method is designed to discover a diverse set of high-quality theory hypotheses. We test the method in a complex phenomenological environment comprising 132 observables across several sectors of particle physics, including 24 measurements that deviate from their SM predictions by more than $1.5σ$. We show that the learned RL policy efficiently constructs multi-operator theories that substantially improve the global fit. Comparisons with independent random-subset and random-sequential searches demonstrate the ability of the RL policy to navigate this large combinatorial space. Our results establish RL as a systematic framework for exploring diverse patterns of new physics (NP) interactions within SMEFT.

##### **Just sort it! A note on the LP behind rank-one anti-loop edits**
2611.21432v1 by Matteo Fischetti

We use the term benchleaking for the case in which a benchmark's own test examples enter training data, so that a score reflects recall rather than capability. First, in post-training, the traces have become explicit: recent audits show an agent stating hidden test requirements it was never given in 23% of its attempts, where its predecessor did so in none, and continuing to follow them after the task is changed to contradict them (Schaeffer and Räuker, 2026; Lo and Lo, 2026). Second, the problem has reached pre-training. Using few-shot continuation on 20 benchmarks across four public base models, we find that some base models write out other test examples---complete questions, options, and answers---that appear nowhere in their input. For the recently released ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base, 96% of HumanEval problems leak with their reference implementations and 85% of OpenBookQA questions leak with their answers, against 9% and none for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base. To quantify what a reported score owes to such examples, we define an Anti-Hack score: the benchmark score recomputed with every leaked example marked wrong and the number of examples unchanged. Averaged over the suite, it costs DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base 1.5 points and Hy3-preview-Base 1.3, against 5.0 for MiMo-V2.5-Base and 16.6 for ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base. Open base models are the foundation of much downstream research, so benchleaking now affects more than leaderboard rankings. We call for decontamination reports and contamination-adjusted scores to become standard practice.

##### **Can AI Mature a Model's Geometry? Training-Free Weight Baking in LLMs**
2611.21310v1 by Amin Golnari, Saeid Sanei

Can an AI make a trained language model more mature, in the sense of a better organized latent space, by looking only at the geometry of that space? An analyst model reads a fixed-size digest of topological descriptors of the layers of a frozen target and proposes a transformation, which closed-form ridge regression writes into the feed-forward weights without gradient training. Using lightweight models, 126 runs show that category purity rose on unseen words and sentences, benchmark accuracy stayed within sampling error, and the analyst matched a category-aware heuristic with gentler edits. In a five-model round robin approach, 16 pairs raised purity and four left the target unchanged. The results provide a baseline for future work on LLM-guided, training-free model editing without using raw weights.

##### **Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs**
2611.21307v1 by Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis

Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. In practice, those labels are produced by an executable algorithm for the behavior, written before studying the model supposed to implement it. However, the criterion these methods optimize only leverages the answer an intervention should produce. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. On the MIB benchmark PSI performs on par with supervised alignment at matched data and training budget, without curated pairs, and it needs no ground-truth labels; while on natural science questions with gold labels withheld, it tracks the model's own answers better than a subspace trained on gold labels. The declaration also fixes a coordinate system with known semantics, enabling donor-free steering: jointly training PSI for both localization and control realizes 94.8% of held-out requests for answers the investigator chooses, without the need for donor prompts.

Five approaches were compared: U-Net, pix2pix, conditional diffusion, an error-conditioned model, and a mixture of error-specific specialists. Evaluation used image similarity and geometric metrics across three test sets. All approaches improved mean image similarity and reduced geometric errors, with U-Net and the two error-aware approaches outperforming pix2pix and conditional diffusion.

We therefore rebuild seven test sets from pinned sources and score twelve systems under one protocol. Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing mathematical discovery, moving beyond benchmark-level problem solving to tackle frontier research problems. With SDKs and agent frameworks, developers connect these components through supporting code that prepares model inputs, propagates state, and dispatches model-selected steps. Workflow changes can require coordinated updates to this code, increasing development and testing effort. Splitting execution logic between application code and framework internals also limits compiler visibility into control flow and state dependencies, hindering automatic optimization. We introduce Agent-Native Programming, which unifies these three components with shared execution semantics. We realize this approach in Jac, a Python-superset language, through "by llm()" for typed model operations and tool use, Object-Spatial Programming for workflow structure and state, and "visit ... by llm()" for model-guided routing. The routing construct derives the model's choices from executable workflow steps and schedules the selected steps. This explicit connection between model decisions and program execution gives the compiler and runtime a common basis for dependency analysis and automatic optimization. Across five benchmarks and five baselines, Jac achieves a 1.8-8.7x reduction in AI-integration code relative to the OpenAI SDK, with competitive observed task quality and lower coding-agent development effort. ##### **AnachroBench: Evaluating Historical Consistency in Large Vision-Language Models**

To address this gap, we propose AnachroBench, a controlled discriminative benchmark for evaluating historical consistency in visual scenes. It contains 1,600 image pairs from eight countries spanning the late 19th to the early 20th century, each pairing an original historical image with an anachronistic version containing a seamlessly inserted modern object. AnachroBench evaluates three aspects: Inserted Object Perception, which tests whether models detect the inserted modern object; Era-Country Consistency, which assesses whether the scene is consistent with the specified era and country; and Conditional Era-Country Consistency, which measures consistency judgments only when the inserted object is correctly perceived. A false consistency judgment occurs when a model perceives the modern object but still judges the scene as historically consistent. Overall, AnachroBench reveals a gap between inserted object perception and era-country consistency judgments, providing a targeted evaluation of this behavior.

##### **The Price of Choosing Examples: Context Overfitting in Adaptive In-Context Learning**
2611.21251v1 by Xiaoyu Li, Jiangxuan Long

Finally, we instantiate the theory for Bayesian linear in-context regression, including an order-statistics analysis of query-aligned top-$k$ Gaussian retrieval. ##### **A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications**

Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. ##### **Oriented Semi-DETR for Semi-Supervised Aerial Object Detection**
2611.21223v1 by Yuqiu Li, Jian Xue, Fan Li, Ke Lu

Semi-supervised object detection has recently been extended to DETR, where oriented boxes introduce an ambiguity that does not arise for axis-aligned boxes: the map from parameters to rectangles is two-to-one, since $(c_x,c_y,w,h,θ)$ and $(c_x,c_y,h,w,θ+π/2)$ denote the same rotated rectangle. Losses and matching costs are computed from the parameters, so two boxes that coincide in space can be far apart in the metric that drives training. A correctly localized student box is then penalized by the regression loss, and the inflated matching cost leads the Gaussian mixture model (GMM) filter to discard it. We propose Oriented Semi-DETR, which removes this ambiguity wherever a pseudo box enters training. Swap-aligned Target Selection regresses towards the encoding closer to the student prediction. A Swap-invariant Reliability Cost minimizes over the two encodings inside the matching cost, so the filter decides on geometry alone. Swap-aware Pseudo-label Denoising starts denoising earlier, ramps its loss weight linearly, and weights each query by the teacher score and encoding margin. All three are parameter-free and require no additional forward pass. Across all label ratios of DOTA-v2.0, Oriented Semi-DETR outperforms DETR-based supervised and semi-supervised baselines, with the largest gains at the strict IoU threshold.

Version 0.1.0 holds 35,177 prescription labels that carry a patient text, 21,416 distinct patient texts, 250,829 patient-text segments matched to prescriber sections through their regulatory headings, reading-level measures on both sides, and rule-based counts of plain-language wording. The same guides measured on the flattened text that the bulk files serve read about two grades higher, a difference that comes from lost list and heading boundaries. Patient texts address the reader in 41 of every 100 sentences, against 0.2 in prescriber sections. A blind hand check of 100 pairs supports the heading-based alignment for seven of ten heading types. ##### **K2-Horizon: A Frontier Fleet of Open Source Language Models**
2611.21003v1 by  K2 Team, Zhengzhong Liu, Haonan Li, Xuezhe Ma, Alexander Moreno, Subham Sekhar Sahoo, Rupesh Kumar Srivastava, Mikhail Yurochkin, Hadi Abdine, Yash Akhauri, Adharsh Babu, David Bellamy, Jalaj Bhandari, Dani Bouch, Wail Bouhedja, Weili Cao, Ahmad Chamma, Po-Chun Chang, Junlin Chen, Lingjie Chen, Zhoujun Cheng, Kyle Chickering, Ryandito Diandaru, Evan Dufraisse, Chaitanya Dwivedi, Bilal El Bouardi, Desai Fan, Richard Fan, Chengqian Gao, Rifo Ahmad Genadi, Jonathan Geuter, Charles Guille-Escuret, Willis Guo, Dhawal Gupta, Seungwook Han, Tingxu Han, Shibo Hao, Besher Hassan, Guowei He, Ming Shan Hee, Joel Hestness, Yu Huang, Yuan Huang, Haolong Jia, Linghao Jin, Junu Kim, Lingjing Kong, Nurkhan Laiyk, Andrej Leban, Liqun Ma, John Maggs, Amr Mohamed, Mukhesh Narra, Omkar Pangarkar, Aldo Pareja, Khiem Pham, Varad Pimpalkhute, Nikhil Ranjan, Elizaveta Rebrova, Hector Ren, Shaurya Rohatgi, Guokan Shang, Jianshu She, Chufan Shi, Aaryamonvikram Singh, Daria Soboleva, Kartik Sreenivasan, Suqi Sun, Yuekai Sun, Bowen Tan, Liping Tang, Shengkun Tang, Muzi Tao, Mallikarjuna Tupakula, Micah Villmow, Hongyi Wang, Huijuan Wang, Renxi Wang, Yongxin Wang, Yuqi Wang, Zhenting Wang, Shicheng Wen, Yingquan Wu, Anze Xie, Andrew Xing, Matthew Yang, Shuanghai Yu, Kerem Zaman, Zenan Zhai, Hongli Zhan, Xuezhou Zhang, Anshun Asher Zheng, Zhe Zhou, Richard Zhuang, Eric P. Xing

We present K2-Horizon, a fleet of six fully open language models, from a 0.9B dense model to a 375B-A23B MoE, built for general-purpose reasoning and agentic work across math and science, software engineering, deep research, office automation, and beyond. The models lead or are competitive with strong open models of their size on agentic and coding benchmarks. On the modeling side, to strengthen reasoning, we introduce Mixture-of-Value Attention (MoVA), applying MoE to attention value projections, and softplus attention gating, a more flexible, theoretically motivated gate. Our 36B-A4B model uses both and nearly matches a 32B dense model trained on the same data, with one-seventh the active parameters. We also introduce Uno, lossless diffusion adapters that speed up decoding by up to 2.2x without a separate draft model. Our release follows the LLM360 definition of open source for transparency and reproducibility: the full development history, including intermediate checkpoints from every training stage, pre-training data, training logs, and all training, evaluation, and infrastructure code, notably our production-scale xLLM framework for dense and MoE training and the RL360 reinforcement-learning stack. Beyond the data itself, we release and document in detail every pipeline that built it, the part of model development that open releases most rarely disclose. These pipelines produced roughly 10T synthetic pre-training tokens, showing that synthetic data can be a major ingredient of pre-training at scale. Four of the models share one 22T-token pre-training corpus and recipe, revealing consistent scaling behavior and enabling controlled studies of scale and sparsity. All artifacts are available at https://ifm.ai/k2/

摘要：

##### **Does the Voice Matter? Evaluating Frustration Detection on Deployed Clinical Voice-Agent Calls**
2611.21001v1 by Annika Michael, Zachary Ellis, Yuxiang Zhou, Samuel Thio, Yajie Vera He, Spencer Hazel, Jared Joselowitz

Autonomous voice agents now conduct routine clinical follow-up calls, so some automated triage is unavoidable. A patient becoming frustrated is one thing that triage should catch. These systems typically read only the transcript the speech-to-text stage produces, not the voice, and whether that is enough is largely untested on real clinical speech. We study per-turn frustration detection on real patient calls from a deployed clinical voice agent: 150 turns from six calls labelled by three annotators, with a length-matched acted corpus as a control, and 714 turns from 30 calls. Patient turns are overwhelmingly short: across the deployed calls, roughly 59% are one or two words, against 13% in the full acted corpus, and a corpus accuracy gap remains even after the acted set is length-matched. On one-word turns, transcript-only detection does not exclude chance. Across five audio-capable language models, the transcript flags 3 or 4 of the 39 frustrated one-word turns in the 30 calls, and a classifier given only the word count reaches 57.3%. Whether audio helps depends on the model and on the instruction. Asked to attend to delivery, two Gemini 2.5 models gain 3.0 pp over the transcript across 30 calls (-1.0 to 7.5 pp). Of the turns they confidently clear, 6.9% were in fact frustrated for audio against 13.0% for the transcript, a difference of 6.1 pp (2.2 to 10.4 pp). Across the 30 calls, given one prompt identical for both inputs, no model's audio-text interval excludes zero. Under the input-specific prompts, a newer Gemini model and two smaller open models do worse with audio than with the transcript (-3.5, -8.6 and -10.6 pp). A WavLM probe trained on the clinical calls matches the best prompted model overall (71.6%) but misses every frustrated one-word turn in the 24 added calls. When a patient's turn is short, the transcript alone is not enough to rule out frustration.

摘要：

##### **Explaining Cross-Label Decision Structure in Multi-Output Tree Ensembles**
2611.20996v1 by Sylvio Barbon Junior, Hiago F. F. Oliveira, Rafael Figueira Gonçalves, Jessica Fernandes Lopes, Ricardo Cerri

A multi-output tree ensemble predicts several binary labels through decision paths that it largely shares across those labels, yet the attribution methods commonly used to explain such models treat each output in isolation. We propose multi-sink Decision Predicate Graphs (ms-DPGs), which extend decision predicate graphs with one sink per label: each leaf contributes to the sink of every label its in-bag positive count for that label. On this representation we define Shared Predicate Flow (SPF), the share of a label's decision mass that passes through conditions active for both labels of a pair, and a contracted view of the graph in which label-specific lifts indicate whether a shared condition raises the probability of both labels or of one at the expense of the other. Controlled experiments show that SPF captures shared decision conditions rather than mere feature overlap. On nine multi-label benchmarks, native multi-output forests share substantially more decision structure than Binary Relevance on eight, including five of the six on which the two are at predictive parity, even at equal hyperparameters, whereas per-label TreeSHAP rankings register the change of architecture without showing whether more is shared. Because a native forest routes every label through the same splits, SPF describes the fitted model rather than the data, and on eight of the nine benchmarks it rises when the feature-label signal is removed; a high SPF is therefore not evidence of shared predictive information. ms-DPG offers a global, predicate-level account of how a multi-output ensemble reuses decision conditions across its labels.

摘要：

##### **Hivemind: A Federated Protocol for AI-Mediated Tacit Knowledge Sharing in Organisations**
2611.20988v1 by Srinivasan Sundaresan

This paper presents Hivemind, a federated protocol that enables AI agents to exchange tacit knowledge on behalf of their users, asynchronously and without requiring any direct human interaction. Individual AI memory systems capture the decisions, reasoning, and contextual knowledge that surface during AI-assisted work. Hivemind creates the shared space in which this captured knowledge can flow between individuals across team and departmental boundaries, addressing a structural gap that neither centralised knowledge repositories nor existing AI tooling closes, even where partial solutions such as expert-finding systems exist. The paper evaluates centralised versus decentralised design approaches, proposes a federated architecture grounded in Nonaka and Takeuchi's (1995) Socialisation, Externalisation, Combination and Internalisation (SECI) model, and demonstrates a proof-of-concept implementation using Design Science Research (DSR) methodology. Measuring the enterprise benefit of federated tacit expertise sharing remains an open task for future work.

摘要：

##### **Calpha: Toward Native Biomolecular Intelligence in a General-Purpose Language Model**
2611.20986v1 by Yong Liu, Zhanpeng Shi, Peicong Lin, Zhenyi Zhong, Yizhou Dang, Wendi Wu, Haoling Li, Shuangjia Zheng

Biomolecular foundation models predict the structures of proteins and their complexes accurately, and large language models (LLMs) reason in natural language, but the two remain separate: the abilities of the former are not part of a general-purpose LLM, and the reasoning of the latter does not enter a structure prediction. Calpha aims at one general model that folds proteins and complexes, understands protein sequence and structure, and lets its language reasoning influence the structures it predicts. This report covers the first step, in which an LLM outputs single-chain protein structures. Qwen3.5-9B reads a sequence enriched with ESM2 residue features. It can respond in text or emit a token that invokes a pretrained SimpleFold structure head, which receives information about the protein only through Qwen's residue states. Under a fixed folding request, the head achieves mean TM-scores of 0.808 on the FoldBench-334 development set, 0.635 on CASP16 domains and 0.900 on a CAMEO-style PDB set. We compare these predictions with a baseline in which ESM2-3B encodes the sequence and a fine-tuned SimpleFold-3B head predicts the coordinates. The two systems also differ in trainable weights, training duration and crop length. For chains of 100--299 residues, all three paired confidence intervals include zero. At 300--600 residues, the mean differences are -0.044, -0.160 and -0.025, respectively. In a separate test, four English request types triggered folding on every evaluated example. Protein training reduced accuracy on a 350-item MMLU-Pro subset from 77.4% to 70.3%. An LLM can therefore output single-chain structures from its own states, with lower long-chain scores in the current comparisons and reduced accuracy on the evaluated MMLU-Pro subset. Complex prediction, structure understanding and the effect of language reasoning on the predicted coordinates remain future work.

摘要：

##### **GRACE: Parallel Best-of-N Multi-Agent Analysis of Closed-Form Questions over Dataframes with Guardrail Selection and Adaptive Retry**
2611.20983v1 by Fabio Picariello, Salvatore Cipolla, Susanna Bonura, Vito Morreale

LLM agents answer closed-form questions over a tabular dataframe by writing and executing Python, almost always as a single rollout: one interpretation, one script, one result, accepted without comparison. The few pipelines that produce several candidates reconcile them by answer-agreement voting, which needs a single comparable scalar and always returns one, even when every candidate is wrong. We present GRACE (Generative, Ranked, Adaptive Code Ensemble), a production-oriented pipeline that instead runs parallel best-of-N selection specialised to dataframe code. N agents run concurrently under a strict, machine-checkable output contract, diversified by persona hints, per-retry rotation, and a two-regime temperature schedule. Rather than vote, GRACE judges the executed artefacts: a pairwise tournament pre-filters the pool and a domain-specialised guardrail either selects the best candidate or, when none is defensible, rejects the whole pool and abstains with a diagnosis (an option voting structurally lacks), which in turn drives an adaptive retry with per-agent feedback. On InfiAgent-DABench (DAEval, M = 246), using Qwen3.6-35B-A3B as the backbone, GRACE raises answer match from 39% for a single-rollout baseline to 71% (+32 pp, 1.8x), recovering 53% of previously wrong answers. On the same DAEval task, the smaller dense Qwen3.5-9B lifts answer match from 38% to 63% (+25 pp, 1.7x). A controlled 3 x 3 study that sweeps the number of parallel agents against the retry budget finds, on the 35B backbone, the two levers largely substitutable: once either one is generous, the other adds little, identifying a cheap operating point for deployment. The same directional pattern replicates on the independent DataBench benchmark, again on the Qwen3.6-35B-A3B backbone, where answer match rises by 15 percentage points (from 70% to 85%) across 400 sampled questions.

摘要：

##### **Breaking feature collapse in self-supervised time series encoders**
2611.20981v1 by Özgün Turgut, Daniel Rueckert

General-purpose time series encoders promise a single backbone that generalises across tasks and domains; however, they currently do not scale. We trace this down to a critical point of failure: under the predominant masked data modelling supervision, the encoder's output tokens partially collapse. This is the natural consequence of supervising encoders only in the input space, which leaves no constraint against redundant token dimensions in the feature space. We introduce OTISv2, which suppresses the collapse by complementing the reconstruction objective in input space with two objectives in the feature space: a self-distillation loss against an exponential-moving-average teacher, and a KoLeo regulariser. Under matched training, the resulting encoder reduces the cosine similarity and lifts the effective rank of its output tokens relative to its predecessor, and it scales monotonically from 7.1 M to 116 M parameters where its predecessor declines. It matches or surpasses existing general-purpose time series encoders across 143 uni- and multi-variate datasets. To foster further research, we release our code and pre-trained weights.

摘要：

##### **Jev-as-a-Reward: Validating Probabilistic Safety Judges for Reinforcement Learning**
2611.20955v1 by Eunkyeong Lee, Junhyung Moon, Seunggwan Hong, Dongsuk Oh

Probabilistic safety judges return the probability of a safety label and cheaply score reinforcement learning rollouts. A confidence gate sends only uncertain verdicts to a stronger LLM judge, which leaves confidently passed harm in a blind spot. Policies can exploit this blind spot, and commercial judges release new versions. We propose a protocol that validates such a judge before and during training, separates the share of confidently passed harm from its amount, and re-validates it after each judge change. We apply it to two versions of Jev, a commercial decision-only judge, and to an open guard classifier. Under the probability reward of Jev, passed harm shrinks in all 11 runs under the primary reference, mostly more slowly than caught harm, and concentrates in the blind spot. Binarizing this judge meets the escape criterion on Llama, and the open guard classifier shows an escape signal in one seed under a second reference. Guard classifiers share this blind spot, and even frontier LLM judges catch at most one of 10 human-labeled confident misses. Without a trained reward model, the probability reward with a helpfulness term lowers HarmBench attack success on Llama-3.1-8B from 0.287 to at most 0.033 at the final checkpoint.

摘要：

##### **ReAccess: Look Once, Stay Focused with Efficient Test Time Memory**
2611.20936v1 by Tianhao Chen, Yilong Fan, Yuheng Wu, Xiaobin Hu, Dongman Lee

Despite recent advances, vision-language models (VLMs) still struggle with fine-grained visual understanding, often failing to effectively utilize critical evidence already encoded in their visual representations. Existing approaches address this limitation through repeated visual processing or explicit reasoning, introducing substantial computational overhead. In this paper, we propose ReAccess, a lightweight framework that preserves and reuses question-relevant visual evidence through compact associative memory. Constructed during a single visual prefill, the memory supports progressive evidence retrieval that steers both attention queries and hidden representations, enabling effective visual grounding without repeated image encoding or explicit reasoning traces. Extensive experiments demonstrate consistent improvements in both general visual understanding and fine-grained perception, while achieving substantially faster inference than existing reasoning-intensive approaches, demonstrating a promising path to efficient multimodal reasoning.

摘要：

##### **Razavi-Bench: Tracking AI Progress in Analog Circuit Reasoning**
2611.20935v1 by Zhishuai Zhang, Oscar Toomey, Taekwang Jang

We propose Razavi-Bench, a benchmark built from the 50 questions in Razavi's two Analog Design Experiments With AI articles and graded by GPT-6 Luna against reference answers and rubrics, and use it to track how fast AI models improve at analog circuit analysis. Across 73 multimodal large language models released between May 2024 and November 2026, the top score rose from 28.83% to 98.33% in 30 months, mostly before the reference answers were published. The leading models now answer almost every textbook question correctly; the hardest questions show a circuit that resembles a textbook one but works differently, or ask about behavior the obvious analysis misses. Within two and a half years, a set of questions that exposed the limits of AI models has become one they answer almost perfectly.

摘要：

##### **Advancing Cognitive Pairwise Comparisons for Human-Centric Artificial Intelligence and Data Science: A Human-in-the-Loop Framework**
2611.20926v1 by Kevin Kam Fung Yuen

Human-centric artificial intelligence and data science (HCAD) systems increasingly involve human-in-the-loop (HITL) interventions to overcome the limitations of fully automated machine learning algorithms, such as automation bias and the inability to incorporate tacit domain knowledge, and continuously improve predictive performance. Data transformations, feature selection, feature weighting, and model selection usually require laborious human involvement across iterations of artificial intelligence and data science (AI&DS) pipelines, making development a challenging and time-consuming process. The Cognitive Pairwise Comparison (CPC) framework is proposed as a human-centric methodology to elicit preferences and priorities in a structured format. To establish essential facets of human-centricity, this research advances the CPC methodology so that user preference elicitation can be systematically quantified in these challenging AI&DS tasks. To demonstrate the usability and validity of these facets, this study presents several applications within movie recommender systems using the MovieLens dataset. It exemplifies how the extended CPC framework can facilitate interactions between human judgments and data across supervised learning, dimensionality reduction, and unsupervised learning algorithms.

摘要：

##### **Navital Gym: Rule-Graded Agent Environments for Indian Workflows**
2611.20924v1 by Daksh Bathla

AI agents are being deployed on Indian workflows such as payments, tax, forms, travel and customer support in Hindi and Hinglish. Public agent evaluations rarely cover these workflows, and many score with another language model. We present Navital Gym, a set of six open environments with 321 public tasks in five task families: trip planning, UPI payments and disputes, GST invoicing and returns, form filling (including a multi-document split) and customer support in Hindi, Hinglish and three experimental Indian languages. Every task is graded by named, deterministic rules that recompute the outcome from a synthetic sandbox world. No language model judges any score. We report one run per task for three models on all six environments, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Claude Haiku 4.5, and for Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite on two (UPI and the Support core). Opus solves 315 of 321 tasks, Sonnet 302 and Haiku 205. Trip planning and Hindi/Hinglish support separate the models most. We also describe a private held-out set that is not published, and a 7B adapter trained in the gym. Code is Apache-2.0 and task data is CC BY 4.0. The rules are a documented simplification of Indian practice, not the live systems.

摘要：

##### **Experience Rot: Experienced Agents Fail Where Novices Succeed**
2611.20922v1 by Zhen Xu, Hang Yin, Zitao Zhang, Kevin Zhao, Xuefeng Liu

Agents are increasingly built to learn from experience: they keep conversation histories, memory files, retrieved notes and skills, and reuse what worked before. We show that this experience turns against them when their tools change silently. We test a range of models with tools defined as in the Model Context Protocol (MCP). After a silent change, experienced agents fail on most tasks, while agents new to the task mostly succeed. We call this failure Experience Rot. Experienced agents copy their earlier call instead of checking the current interface, even when the tool definition shows the change. Then they report success. When the documentation is incomplete, experience is what makes agents succeed. After a change, the same experience leaves them worse off than no experience at all. The failure appears in every form of memory we test and grows with the amount of experience. Simple remedies fall short: announcing the update barely helps, and pointing the agent to the documentation fails whenever the documentation does not spell out the answer.

#### Abstracts
##### **TRACE: Topological Representations for Anomaly detection through Contrastive Embeddings**
2611.21548v1 by Sydney Johns

Intrusion detectors must recognize evolving malicious behavior even when confirmed attack labels are scarce or incomplete. We present TRACE, a topology-aware graph contrastive learning framework for label-efficient intrusion detection. TRACE learns from benign system-activity graphs by combining message-passing and topological representations, then assigns anomaly scores in the same projected embedding space optimized by the contrastive objective. This design captures both local interactions and graph-scale structural deviations without using attack labels during representation learning. A labeled validation set containing benign and malicious graphs is used only to calibrate the anomaly threshold. We evaluate TRACE on StreamSpot, GraSec-IoT, and Unicorn Wget against a supervised graph neural network and the benign-only GraphCL baseline. TRACE obtains F1 scores of 0.923, 0.790, and 0.583, respectively. Compared with GraphCL, this is higher on StreamSpot (0.450) and Unicorn Wget (0.203), but lower on GraSec-IoT (0.975). The supervised model leads on StreamSpot and GraSec-IoT, whereas TRACE has the highest F1 on Unicorn Wget. The mixed results show that topology-aware benign-only learning can improve detection in some graph settings but does not provide a uniform advantage.

摘要：

##### **SciResearch-Eval: Evaluating Scientific Judgment in Autonomous Experimental Agents**
2611.21541v1 by Brian T. Weston, Alondra Torres Contreras, Aldair E. Gongora

We introduce SciResearch-Eval, an interactive benchmark of scientific judgment built on a standard laboratory problem: measuring a material's thermal properties by heating it and fitting a heat-conduction simulator. An agent designs its own experiments, uses a supplied simulator that may be under-resolved or physically wrong, and reports each property with a 95% interval. To our knowledge it is the first benchmark to combine agent-designed experiments, a deliberately flawed simulator, and proper scoring of calibrated intervals against exact ground truth. On a held-out split (93 tasks, 9 models, 2,511 episodes), models handle procedural traps. When the simulator is missing physics, most report wrong values while stating they are 95% sure: their ranges contain the truth in 20-62% of cases, against 94% for GPT-6 Astra and 99% for Claude Fable 5.2. At default effort the failing Claude 5.5 models rarely diagnose the missing physics (Opus in 35% of episodes, against 66% for Astra), and even when they do, their ranges often miss; Astra and Fable design experiments in which the flawed simulator is accurate, such as gentle heating, which keeps Astra calibrated even when it does not name the cause. Asking for wider intervals does not help; stating the strategy improves interval scores 1.6-2.5-fold, replicated on a pre-registered fresh split, but coverage barely moves. A pre-registered effort ablation shows that the correction is latent in the Claude 5.5 models: at maximum effort Opus improves more than tenfold and is no longer detectably behind Astra at default, but only with 13-31 times more reasoning; older and open-weight models gain nothing. Fable 5.2 is the most accurate model at default effort (interval score 1.9 times better than Astra's) but writes 6 times as many tokens, and at Astra's token count it falls short. We release the environment, graders and harness.

摘要：

##### **Jetriever: Shifting Agent Memory Retrieval from Similarity to Utility**
2611.21478v1 by Ruizhe Li, Licheng Zhang, Mingxuan Du, Zhendong Mao

A useful memory need not resemble the question it helps answer. Jetriever ranks each stored memory by Jev's decision score for its usefulness to the current question, replacing similarity search with full-pool utility scoring. On InMind's 125 indirect-query tasks, it places the gold exchange first in 84.8% of cases; matched BM25 and MiniLM controls reach 1.6% and 0.0% when indexing user turns. A 20-question comparison separates finding evidence from ranking it: Jev and Qwen3.5 9B each find 90% of gold exchanges by rank 50, while Laya finds 30%; at rank one, Jev reaches 85% and Qwen3.5 9B 40%. On a dimension-proportional random sample of 60 MemoryAgentBench questions, Jetriever's four-dimension score is 55.4%, against 44.3% for ReFind on the same questions. Jev retrieval costs USD 0.00834 per InMind question and USD 0.03103 per MAB question on average. These results show the value of world knowledge, scores that rank decisive memories first, and affordable full-pool scoring.

摘要：

##### **Network Intrusion Detection Adaptability under Spurious Labels: A Noise-Aware Few-Shot Class-Incremental Learning Framework**
2611.21473v1 by Zijian Zhao, Davide Di Monda, Domenico Ciuonzo, Yipeng Wang, Antonio Pescapè

Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDSs) must incrementally adapt to emerging attacks, even when only a limited amount of labeled network traffic is available. Consequently, Few-Shot Class-Incremental Learning (FSCIL)-based NIDSs have gained growing attention. However, existing FSCIL-based NIDSs are highly vulnerable to label noise in newly-observed attack classes. To address this limitation, we propose NAFN, a noise-aware FSCIL-based NIDS framework that reduces the impact of mislabeled data during incremental adaptation. NAFN consists of two components: (i) Noise Detection, which identifies and removes mislabeled biflows using noise-detection algorithms, and (ii) Feature Sampling, which estimates class-conditional embeddings from clean biflows, calibrates them with base-class statistics, and resamples synthetic features to recover the original biflow count. The framework follows the Rethink Few-Shot (RFS) teacher-student paradigm with lightweight nearest-neighbor or logistic regression classifiers, and various pretraining & memory selection strategies. Experiments on CSE-CIC-IDS2018, Edge-IIoT, and IoT-NID datasets show that NAFN effectively preserves prior knowledge and maintains strong incremental performance even under 50% label noise, consistently improving new-class F1-scores over non-denoised baselines, up to +52%, while being time-efficient and robust in cross-network and high-noise incremental settings. The implementation is publicly available to foster reproducibility.

摘要：

##### **Expertise capture in workplace AI**
2611.21456v1 by Andreas Ekelhart, Ahmad Mohsin, Iqbal H. Sarker, Helge Janicke

AI assistants are becoming part of how knowledge workers think and perform, accumulating the prompts, exemplars, memory and interaction histories that encode a worker's professional judgment. This infrastructure typically runs under employer or platform control. We name the resulting process expertise capture, the absorption of worker-applied knowledge and judgment into AI resources under organizational control. We develop a framework of contribution forms, technical residences and transformation families, and show that how far a contribution can be attributed, separated and revoked depends on where it resides and how it has been transformed, properties fixed by system design long before any dispute arises. When a worker departs, the organization retains a capability shaped by their judgment while the worker loses access to it, and the asymmetry compounds as contributions move from inspectable artifacts into derivatives, learned components and successor systems. We outline three measures, write-time provenance, extraction of separable contributions and an executable exit procedure, that make an agreed allocation technically performable, and make explicit what cannot be separated at all.

摘要：

##### **Emergent Steering Beyond Endpoint Alignment in Chemical Reaction Models**
2611.21423v1 by Yili Shen, Kehan Guo, Haomin Zhuang, Xiangliang Zhang

Machine learning models for chemical reaction prediction are usually evaluated by endpoint accuracy, but endpoint correctness alone does not reveal how a model represents chemical transformation or why it fails. We introduce UniRxnAxis, a unified forward-retrosynthesis framework that models both tasks as direction-conditioned endpoint redistribution in a shared latent space. By aligning source states, transformed states, and encoded target endpoints, UniRxnAxis enables directional auditing of learned latent updates. This shared latent geometry allows the learned displacement (from the encoder source state $S_0$ to the decoder-facing transformed state $S_1$) to be decomposed into an endpoint-parallel component and an endpoint-orthogonal residual, which we call emergent steering. Our evaluation shows that emergent steering is functionally important: in retrosynthesis, it recovers most of the model's utility, remains effective under random, shuffled, and prototype controls, and directly affects which bond edits are promoted or suppressed. The residual-over-parallel ordering persists across alignment strengths, after decoder-interface calibration, against angle-matched random axes, and in a second graph-edit architecture, although its magnitude depends on the reference axis and optimization. The decomposition also supports a proxy taxonomy of model-side failures, showing how reaction models can be audited beyond endpoint accuracy.

摘要：

##### **What You Post Is What You Symptom: Estimating Psychometric Networks from Social Media Discourse**
2611.21362v1 by Ştefana-Arina Tăbuşcă, Ana Sabina Uban, Liviu P. Dinu

Symptom networks in psychopathology are estimated almost exclusively from self-report questionnaires, restricting inference to whatever symptom set a given instrument covers. We introduce a framework for symptom-resolved network estimation directly from social media discourse and apply the framework to depression and eating disorders, with a third anorexia corpus as cross-dataset validation. To our knowledge, we are the first to estimate symptom-resolved psychometric networks from social media text, compare them to clinical co-endorsement networks, and replicate transdiagnostic linguistic markers across 3 independently recruited corpora spanning 2 disorders simultaneously. For depression, expression scores correlate with BDI-II totals at $ρ{=}0.434$, 20 of 21 items reach FDR significance, and split-half reliability is 0.935. The Network Comparison Test detects few edge differences between modalities (3-5\% of edges), while bridge symptoms diverge. For eating disorders, the item-level results are exploratory. Identified linguistic features replicate the same direction across all 3 corpora, and they cluster in three construct families.

摘要：

##### **TRIAGE-CPG: A Cross-Model Study of Budgeted Static Malware Triage with Large Language Models**
2611.21331v1 by Paul-Andrei Sava, Alexander Küchler, Daniel Kowatsch, Bernhard Grill, Jennifer Pullman, Yanick Fratantonio, Luca Invernizzi

When triaging unknown binaries, security analysts must decide which samples to clear as benign, flag as malicious, or escalate for deeper analysis. Large Language Models (LLMs) could support these decisions by interpreting decompiled code, but a binary can contain hundreds or thousands of functions, making exhaustive LLM analysis impractical. We study how current LLMs behave when static triage is formulated as budgeted evidence acquisition: which functions they inspect and how they trade off benign clearance, malware detection, and escalation. To enable a controlled comparison, we implement TRIAGE-CPG, a fixed pipeline over a code property graph (CPG) of a decompiled binary. An LLM-free heuristic selects seed functions, an agent expands the resulting evidence dossier under a fixed budget, and a reporting stage produces a benign, suspicious, or malicious verdict together with a behavioral report. We evaluate eight closed-source and open-weight models on 1,000 Windows PE binaries, using a 600-sample test split and the same zero-shot protocol for every model. We assess the ternary verdicts under both a strict binary reading and triage balanced accuracy (tBA), which credits malware that is flagged or escalated and benign samples only when cleared. After analyzing about 40 of a mean 1,000 functions per binary, the highest-scoring model reaches a strict balanced accuracy of 0.805 and a tBA of 0.855, flagging 65% of malware, escalating another 12%, and clearing 94% of benign samples. The models implement different triage policies: high-specificity models produce small, malware-rich escalation queues, whereas recall-oriented models escalate or flag much more broadly. Both closed-source and open-weight models appear across this spectrum. Our results identify no universal deployment configuration: model choice itself determines the clear/flag/escalate trade-off under limited static evidence.

摘要：

##### **Syllable- and Word-Level Evaluation of Tibetan OCR**
2611.21303v1 by Jacob Moore, Paula Lauren

Optical character recognition (OCR) for Tibetan is evaluated predominantly by character error rate (CER). Tibetan marks syllable boundaries but does not mark word boundaries, so a word error rate requires a segmenter. This study evaluates nine recognizer configurations on typeset, woodblock and two handwritten script styles, alone and in combination, and reports syllable- and word-level error rates, to our knowledge the first for Tibetan OCR. The configurations span CTC models, TrOCR encoder-decoders with and without post-training quantization, and a vision-language model, and each is scored under CER, the syllable error rate (SER) and three segmented word error rates (SWER). CER ranks systems largely as the higher-level rates do (Kendall $τ\geq 0.833$), with one pair ordered oppositely. On the effect of joint training, CER and SER record significant changes of opposite sign in 7 of 64 comparisons. SER needs no segmenter and ranks systems as SWER does under two of the three segmenters ($τ\geq 0.889$). The output of each configuration is also tagged for part of speech, annotated for named entities and machine translated, and each result is compared with that obtained on the reference text. As rough guidance, translation chrF of 60 is reached at SER of 0.047 or below and part-of-speech preservation of 0.8 at 0.078 or below, while no error rate separates the systems that reach entity preservation of 0.6 from those that do not.

摘要：

##### **Latent Bridge: Asking for Help Without Words**
2611.21301v1 by Qichao Wang, Tingyu Qu, Pengcheng Wu, Yiran Zhong, Feida Zhu, Zhiqi Shen, Peilin Zhao, Steven Hoi

Text-based model collaboration requires a large model to generate intermediate responses before a smaller model can use its guidance, adding large-model decoding cost to inference. We introduce POND (Prefill-Only, No-Decode), a method that uses a frozen large sender for prefill and a small receiver for all autoregressive decoding. A trainable bridge maps the sender's hidden states to additional attention keys and values, allowing the receiver to use these representations throughout generation. POND also supports periodic review, in which the sender processes the receiver's partial response and updates its guidance through additional prefill passes while preserving the receiver's existing KV cache. Across reasoning and coding benchmarks, POND improves receiver performance with senders from different model families. In particular, GLM-5.3 provides gains comparable to those of Qwen3.8-Max when paired with a Qwen receiver, supporting the effectiveness of representation transfer between independently trained model families. POND further demonstrates its ability to generalize across domains. When trained on math and coding tasks, it transfers directly to general knowledge tasks without additional domain-specific training. These results demonstrate that large frontier model prefill representations can improve small-model generation without requiring large-model autoregressive decoding.

摘要：

##### **Turing Agent: Agentic Formal Verification for Mathematical Research**
2611.21299v1 by Yang Chen, Qianyi Wang, Ronghui Yang, Zhanhao Li, Yu Cong, Chen Zhang, Hangfan Zhang, Shao Zhang, Shuai Li, Toru Takisaka, Chao Xu, Yi Zhou, Zhihao Tang, Xiao Wang, Bo Zhang, Shuyue Hu

Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing mathematical discovery, moving beyond benchmark-level problem solving to tackle frontier research problems. This progress is ushering mathematics into an era of proof abundance, raising a fundamental challenge: how can we ensure the correctness of the ever-growing volume of AI-generated proofs and transform them into trustworthy, machine-verified mathematical knowledge? We introduce Turing Agent, an agent harness designed to orchestrate end-to-end formal verification of research-level mathematics. Its wo-scale architecture integrates theorem formalization and proof formalization agents, empowered by structured proof decomposition, parallel verification, accumulated proof experience, and selective semantic review. Leveraging our collaborations with theoretical computer science (TCS) researchers, we focus our evaluation on TCS, a particularly demanding domain characterized by intricate quantification, complex dependencies, and stringent semantic requirements. Against direct generation and general-purpose coding harnesses, including Codex CLI and Claude Code, Turing Agent achieves approximately 57-62% faithful formalization success on Formal TCS Bench and 83% overall pass@1 on a TCS-focused subset of RLMEval, using GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro. Notably, Turing Agent, built on DeepSeek, successfully completes the formal verification of three previously unresolved TCS tasks from OpenAI's October 2026 collection. One particularly challenging TCS problem required about a hundred formally verified auxiliary claims. Moreover, by actively exploring and refining the formalization process, Turing Agent can uncover simpler alternative proofs, expose unnecessary dependencies in original proofs, and generate reusable mathematical knowledge as a byproduct of verification.

##### **A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications**
2611.21232v1 by Mo Zhou

Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Cloud-based assistants face governance limitations in data transmission, continuous availability, and the reliability of physical actions, which are especially salient in households where devices remain online, data accumulate over time, and commands trigger real-world actions. Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. The article proposes a five-dimensional evaluation framework covering architectural capability, system resources, privacy, safety and reliability, and human experience, together with six design principles and a future research agenda for local-first intelligent home systems.

##### **Validating LLM-Based Essay Scorers: A Comparative Analysis in Critical Online Reasoning in Medical Education**

Automated essay scorers (AES) based on large language models (LLMs) promise human-level accuracy. However, training, fine-tuning, and evaluating LLMs for specific assessment tasks is costly. In particular, the question arises whether these AES should be trained for specific domains or whether models based on generic LLMs might be sufficient. Human annotations, by contrast, are often more valid but far more expensive. This study evaluates whether ensembles of AES systems based on general-purpose LLMs can approximate human ratings of medical-education writing tasks by leveraging encoded linguistic and conceptual knowledge. The goal is to assess whether general-purpose models are applicable for assisting or potentially replacing human annotation in this domain. We experiment with 11 AES systems and 8 different LLMs and evaluate 668 student responses to 12 tasks, using \numprint{2076} evaluations by 7 human annotators. These ratings are aggregated to create consistent performance levels that can be used as target classes for classifiers trained on AES outputs. Our results show that learner classes based on human ratings can be accurately reconstructed using a linear SVM trained on a small set of AES features. A single classifier generalized well across tasks and domains, with reasoning and content features being most predictive. This suggests that out-of-the-box LLMs, even without domain-specific fine-tuning, can support automatic essay grading in medical education. While individual AES systems are insufficient alone, their combination approximates human evaluators, pointing to a reduced need for manual scoring or its supplementation with automated methods.

##### **Explaining Cross-Label Decision Structure in Multi-Output Tree Ensembles**
2611.20996v1 by Sylvio Barbon Junior, Hiago F. F. Oliveira, Rafael Figueira Gonçalves, Jessica Fernandes Lopes, Ricardo Cerri

A multi-output tree ensemble predicts several binary labels through decision paths that it largely shares across those labels, yet the attribution methods commonly used to explain such models treat each output in isolation. We propose multi-sink Decision Predicate Graphs (ms-DPGs), which extend decision predicate graphs with one sink per label: each leaf contributes to the sink of every label its in-bag positive count for that label. On this representation we define Shared Predicate Flow (SPF), the share of a label's decision mass that passes through conditions active for both labels of a pair, and a contracted view of the graph in which label-specific lifts indicate whether a shared condition raises the probability of both labels or of one at the expense of the other. Controlled experiments show that SPF captures shared decision conditions rather than mere feature overlap. On nine multi-label benchmarks, native multi-output forests share substantially more decision structure than Binary Relevance on eight, including five of the six on which the two are at predictive parity, even at equal hyperparameters, whereas per-label TreeSHAP rankings register the change of architecture without showing whether more is shared. Because a native forest routes every label through the same splits, SPF describes the fitted model rather than the data, and on eight of the nine benchmarks it rises when the feature-label signal is removed; a high SPF is therefore not evidence of shared predictive information. ms-DPG offers a global, predicate-level account of how a multi-output ensemble reuses decision conditions across its labels.

##### **Hivemind: A Federated Protocol for AI-Mediated Tacit Knowledge Sharing in Organisations**
2611.20988v1 by Srinivasan Sundaresan

This paper presents Hivemind, a federated protocol that enables AI agents to exchange tacit knowledge on behalf of their users, asynchronously and without requiring any direct human interaction. Individual AI memory systems capture the decisions, reasoning, and contextual knowledge that surface during AI-assisted work. Hivemind creates the shared space in which this captured knowledge can flow between individuals across team and departmental boundaries, addressing a structural gap that neither centralised knowledge repositories nor existing AI tooling closes, even where partial solutions such as expert-finding systems exist. The paper evaluates centralised versus decentralised design approaches, proposes a federated architecture grounded in Nonaka and Takeuchi's (1995) Socialisation, Externalisation, Combination and Internalisation (SECI) model, and demonstrates a proof-of-concept implementation using Design Science Research (DSR) methodology. Measuring the enterprise benefit of federated tacit expertise sharing remains an open task for future work.

##### **Living Context Graphs: Continual Semantic Learning for Enterprise Data Reasoning**
2611.20976v1 by Varun Agrawal, Ayush Srivastava, Eshan Gujar, Kaushal Kumar, Ayush Gupta

Enterprise AI can now perform analytics, reporting, and data-driven workflows, yet the data foundations it relies on remain hard to operationalize: business meaning, relationships, metric definitions, and reporting conventions are absent from database schemas and scattered across undocumented systems and tribal knowledge. We introduce the Living Context Graph (LCG), a continually learned context layer that makes existing enterprise data operational for AI without requiring a copy or transformation of the underlying warehouse. LCG bootstraps base semantics and relationships from the data itself, builds a concept graph that indexes business context as queries are served, writes durable semantic learnings from verified outcomes, and withdraws or re-earns context when later outcomes contradict prior routes. We evaluate LCG through text-to-SQL, a core data-reasoning task, on a transfer-aware split of three BIRD databases comprising 499 questions: 353 form the learning history and 146 are held out and never used to update the context layer. Bootstrapping alone improves overall accuracy by +4.6 percentage points. Learning only from the history raises accuracy on the untouched held-out transfer set by +22.6 points (95% CI [+14.4, +30.8]), exceeding five-shot retrieval from the same history by +12.3 points. Incremental learning reaches the one-shot endpoint within observed evaluation noise on every database. Ablating the graph changes held-out accuracy by at most one question per database but increases per-query cost 2.3-3.3x and median latency 1.8-3.4x: continually learned semantics drive accuracy, while the graph makes that context efficient to serve.

##### **Advancing Cognitive Pairwise Comparisons for Human-Centric Artificial Intelligence and Data Science: A Human-in-the-Loop Framework**
2611.20926v1 by Kevin Kam Fung Yuen

Human-centric artificial intelligence and data science (HCAD) systems increasingly involve human-in-the-loop (HITL) interventions to overcome the limitations of fully automated machine learning algorithms, such as automation bias and the inability to incorporate tacit domain knowledge, and continuously improve predictive performance. Data transformations, feature selection, feature weighting, and model selection usually require laborious human involvement across iterations of artificial intelligence and data science (AI&DS) pipelines, making development a challenging and time-consuming process. The Cognitive Pairwise Comparison (CPC) framework is proposed as a human-centric methodology to elicit preferences and priorities in a structured format. To establish essential facets of human-centricity, this research advances the CPC methodology so that user preference elicitation can be systematically quantified in these challenging AI&DS tasks. To demonstrate the usability and validity of these facets, this study presents several applications within movie recommender systems using the MovieLens dataset. It exemplifies how the extended CPC framework can facilitate interactions between human judgments and data across supervised learning, dimensionality reduction, and unsupervised learning algorithms.

##### **Which AI Use Cases Deserve a Pilot? A Risk and Value Framework for Funding, Scoping, and Scaling AI**
2611.20894v1 by Vishal Srivastava

A composite score can rank AI use cases while leaving the permitted operating scope and the evidence required for expansion unspecified. We treat the unit of decision as a use case at a proposed scope (authority, human mediation, limits, volume, environment, population) and define three decisions: screen the scope, fund the experiment, and authorize expansion only when residual risk is acceptable and evidence of incremental net value applies at that scope. A risk track (R1-R4) is implemented as an explicit decision table with vetoes and documented precedence, and a value track follows four evidence stages with each transition needing its own evidence. Funding uses expected value of perfect information as an upper bound and the value of sample information for the specific experiment. A quantified expected-loss check, required where policy says so, computes the containment an expansion needs and credits a control only on evidence measured at the proposed scope; in a synthetic scenario the highest-value control design fails that test while a smaller scope passes. A reproducible Python package with 279 passing tests demonstrates the framework on synthetic refund, reconciliation and knowledge-assistant use cases, including a synthetic matched-scope fixture and a transferability stress test (value evidence is simulated, not realized), and compares it with a public AWS sample. Thresholds and appetite are prototype policy requiring field validation.
