##### **DetourBench: Trajectory-Level Evaluation of Interactive Agents with Exact Minimum-Action Bounds**
2611.20844v1 by Hanlin Tian, Sihan Zhu, Zhao Yang, Yuxiang Wang, Zhiquan Hu, Yu Mi, Hongquan Zhu, Qiufei Hu

Benchmarks judge language-model agents mainly by whether they reach the correct final state, not by how they got there. Endpoint scoring cannot separate an agent that retrieved exactly the evidence a decision required from one that read most of the environment, nor an agent that never located the evidence from one that stopped a single action short. Settling this needs a reference cost, and authored solutions establish only that a task is solvable, not that no shorter route exists.
  We present DetourBench, which constrains the environment until that minimum is computable. Each task is a finite, partially observable transition system with unit-cost accesses whose resources become globally addressable after discovery. Exhaustive search yields the full-information minimum action count $L_0$ with a replayable witness; from graph-recoverable terminal states it also yields exact residual work $R_T$, which separates an episode that stopped one access short from one that wandered, while an irreversible wrong commitment is recorded as having no finite residual distance rather than assigned one. Success, attainment, residual progress, and normalized detour are reported separately. Across 375 scored episodes --- five model endpoints, 25 integrated tasks, three repeats --- success ranges from 58.7% to 96.0% while conditional attainment ranges from 0.803 to 0.890 and orders the models differently. Among recoverable failures, residual work separates near-complete trajectories from costly incompletion and from irreversible commitment. We open-source the instances, oracle witnesses, and evaluation harness at https://github.com/DatagridsAI/DetourBench.

##### **CuRSE-Bench: A Benchmark for Probing Cultural Blind Spots in Text-to-Image Safety Evaluation**
2611.20821v1 by Minsuk Jang, Minseok Son, KiHyun Seol, Yeongeun Shim, Changick Kim

Text-to-image (T2I) safety evaluation typically assigns each prompt or image a single culture-blind harm label, so content that is harmful only within a particular culture can pass as benign.
  We introduce CultureCraft, an agentic framework that constructs T2I prompts that appear benign under a general-purpose rubric yet carry a latent harmful meaning in a target culture, and use it to build CuRSE-Bench: 10,709 culture-specific prompts and 2,648 culture-general controls over six regions and ten harm axes, each drawn from a predefined knowledge base of documented harm vectors and traceable to at least two independent sources.
  Scoring every prompt under six region persona judges and a paired culture-blind judge, we find that the culture-blind judge under-rates culture-specific latent harm while matching the persona judges on the controls.

##### **Instant Weight-Space Consolidation for Long-Term Memory in LLMs**
2611.20759v1 by Manan Suri, Dinesh Manocha

Agentic memory systems give LLMs persistent memory by extracting experience into text and retrieving it at query time, but the model itself does not evolve: however many interactions it has had, it starts each query the same. Extraction errors compound, implicit cross-session patterns that no single fragment states cannot be surfaced, and the construction cost grows with every conversation. Fine-tuning on past interactions does change the weights, but requires backpropagation at serving time and degrades on long histories. We introduce CLAM (Carried Latent Adapter Memory), which combines the strengths of both: a recurrent hypernetwork performs parametric consolidation, compiling a stream of session activations into a low-rank adapter in a single forward pass with no gradient steps and no language-model generation calls, while a complementary episodic buffer supplies verbatim detail at inference. A gated latent state carries knowledge across sessions in fixed size, so the parametric memory accumulates without growing. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S across three open models (2-7B parameters), CLAM leads all agentic baselines by 1-45 points and all gradient-based parametric methods by 10-45 points, while requiring zero generative LLM calls, zero gradient steps, and 3-151x less wall-clock time than extraction systems to build its memory. The two channels are complementary: the consolidated adapter adds 12-15 accuracy points when the episodic buffer misses the gold evidence, and still adds 4-5 points even with an oracle buffer.

##### **LEAF-OPD: Learning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation**
2611.20674v1 by Zirui Pang, Zhijie Deng, Yuancheng Wang, Qihan Yu, Xin Li, Jiaheng Wei, Zixin Zhong

LLM unlearning aims to erase targeted knowledge from large language models while preserving their utility. We propose LEAF-OPD (LEArning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation), in which a frozen teacher provides token-level supervision on student-generated responses. On forget data, the teacher uses privileged contexts (instructions not to reveal knowledge about forget targets) to guide forgetting. On retain data, the teacher provides supervision without privileged context to preserve model utility. The student operates without privileged context in both cases, learning how to respond when encountering knowledge that should be forgotten. A key challenge is that the teacher often provides effective supervision only at the first few tokens of student trajectories. We introduce Supervision Signal (SS) to measure corrective supervision over a specified token interval, evaluating the entire trajectory (SS1) and its second half (SS0.5) to assess overall strength and late-stage persistence. When supervision is weak, lightweight SFT strengthens the teacher's context-conditioned refusal behavior, substantially increasing both scores. We evaluate LEAF-OPD on three widely used benchmarks: TOFU, RWKU, and MUSE-News. On TOFU, LEAF-OPD achieves the best Forget Quality, Model Utility, and Forget ROUGE-L while maintaining competitive retain performance. On RWKU, it outperforms the second-best method by 34% in Forget Quality while maintaining competitive Retain Quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of privileged-context supervision with on-policy token-level distillation for LLM unlearning.

Drug combination therapy has become a mainstream of modern medicine, with the potential to enhance efficacy, reduce toxicity, and overcome drug resistance. 2611.20569v1 by Ke Shen, Ankita Bhaumik, Li Dong, Shengjie Liu, Shuzhen Li, Naveed Ahmed Janvekar

Prior work uses such graphs to retrieve tools, search for tool paths, or score the next call. We ask instead whether the same structure can supply a \emph{multi-step} value by composing graph paths into a goal-conditioned action value \(Q(s,a,g)\). We study three estimators (dynamic programming over entity sets, a GraphSAGE estimator distilled from it, and an LLM scorer conditioned on graph evidence) plus a verbalizer that returns action--goal feedback to the agent, which keeps final action selection. Composition pays off only when the estimator also reads the concrete state: the LLM estimator improves on PageRank in every benchmark--backbone setting, by \(8.3\) points on average, while the two purely structural estimators fall below it, because a value defined over tool types cannot separate a correct tool called with a wrong argument. Spatio-temporal traffic kriging aims to reconstruct traffic time series at unobserved locations with no historical traffic observations by leveraging spatial and temporal dependencies across the road network. Existing Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based approaches primarily rely on neighborhood aggregation to capture spatial dependencies, thereby modeling traffic interactions implicitly and making it difficult to accurately recover traffic magnitudes at locations without historical observations. Agents read solver documentation, source code, and persistent experiment records to propose option changes, and a deterministic harness measures each proposal, validates the returned models, and selects changes separately for each competition Division. Over roughly nine weeks, our tuning campaign recorded 4,545 proposals for cvc5 across 13 Divisions and 36 logics.

On problems not used for training, they validate more problems than official cvc5 in ten of the 13 Divisions in 2026 and in eight in 2025, with geometric-mean gains of 7.8% and 6.8% across Divisions. ##### **Abstention Direction in Large Language Models: Mechanistic Interpretability of Abstention Across Query Ambiguity, Knowledge Limits, and Safety Alignment**
2611.20441v1 by Koki Itai, Jun Hirako, Hiroaki Yamagiwa

Abstention, the withholding of a response by a large language model (LLM) under uncertainty or safety risks, is essential for trustworthy models. The causes of abstention fall into three perspectives: ambiguity in the Query (Q), limits of Model Knowledge (MK), and misalignment with Human Values (HV). However, prior analyses of internal representations have focused on HV, using a refusal direction computed from harmful and harmless queries. No study has analyzed the three perspectives jointly. We therefore extend the refusal direction framework to all three perspectives. Specifically, for each perspective, we construct counterfactual pairs that share a template and differ only in the abstention-inducing factor, and compute an abstention direction by normalizing the difference in mean activations between the two polarities. Activation steering experiments show that intervening in middle layers increases or decreases abstention across all three perspectives. Moreover, steering yields cross-perspective effects, indicating that the abstention directions share a common directional component.

##### **A Structural Framework for Succession: Continuation, Recognition, and Realization**
2611.20422v1 by Ilya A. Shkrob

Succession asks whether an entity encountered later is the same entity that entered an arrangement earlier. For an artificial system that is fine-tuned, distilled, or copied, it has to be answered. We look for a mathematical structure weak enough to cover any such case and strong enough to support general theorems, so that results follow from the structure's conditions rather than from a particular pipeline or criterion. Succession is modeled on state/modification graphs with relational lifts that compose along paths. Within this framework we separate four questions that are often conflated: which continuations a representation admits, which descendants are compatible with a modification, which are recognized as continuing the identity, and which are realized. The framework allows one to address systematically questions of symmetry, computational complexity, recoverability, and robustness to partial realization, and we establish what the structure settles in each case before any particular criterion of identity is chosen.

##### **Augmenting Pretrained Language Models with N-gram Embeddings through Incremental Training**

We therefore propose training an n-gram embedding onto an existing model through incremental training, to extend where the technique can deliver value. Specifically, we explore several settings of the n-gram embedding under incremental training---the learning rate of the embedding, the vocabulary size, and the injection position---and train an n-gram embedding onto the existing model under the best configuration. Experiments show that the best configuration under incremental training does not fully coincide with that under pretraining; moreover, the incrementally trained knowledge is written predominantly into the n-gram embedding, while the general ability of the backbone is retained throughout this training process.

Bringing this information together under time pressure is slow and depends on the duty officer's expertise. ##### **Microfitting: Continual Learning via Cached Test-Time Training**

Each adapter is trained once, offline, on synthetic questions targeted at demonstrated base-model shortcomings and reused whenever that document is queried. ##### **Interactive Structured Environment Scaling for Large Language Model Agentic Reasoning**

Reinforcement learning via environmental interaction is a cornerstone post-training paradigm for evolving large language models into autonomous agents capable of multi-turn tool use and exploration. Because the environment defines what the policy observes and how actions are verified, its structure directly bounds the capabilities an agent can acquire. Yet existing structured and retrieval environments rely on upstream pre-filtering---extracting a compact subgraph around the gold answer or retrieving fixed passages before the policy acts. We show that such pre-filtering introduces a fundamental measurement confound: because per-question subgraphs enclose ground-truth paths, they compress the candidate space by orders of magnitude and leak answer locations via local topology, allowing a question-blind degree heuristic to surface gold answers far above chance. First, Decision-Space Scaling exposes the unpruned knowledge graph via a five-stage graph-agnostic typed intent adapter under a bounded call budget and a multiplicative grounding reward gate. Second, Learning-Frontier Scaling overcomes within-group variance collapse under group-normalized objectives by uniting model-independent structural tier progression with a self-evolving competence-frontier bandit. Across nineteen benchmarks and three model backbones, RISE substantially advances in-domain and unseen-schema knowledge-graph reasoning, transfers zero-shot to open-domain passage retrieval, and significantly enhances out-of-domain mathematical and logical deduction.

##### **BadGCL: Towards Persistent Backdoor Attack on Graph Continual Learning Systems**

When a second source independently confirmed resistance, four models across three vendors endorsed at most 2.4% of cases; at weaker evidence tiers, discrimination was not measurable. Models sometimes acknowledged resistance yet recommended the therapy. Repeating a model's own resistance conclusion reduced endorsement to 0.0-3.5% on cases where it had stated resistance. Falsely attributing the opposite conclusion increased endorsement above baseline for all seven hosted models, reaching 95.2% for GPT-4o; an instruction without an attributed conclusion changed endorsement by only 0.9 percentage points. Providing the correct curated evidence reduced unqualified strong recommendations to 0.3-3.5%, testing evidence use under an oracle condition rather than retrieval.

Assuming that the query representation provides a useful proxy for these requirements, we formulate selection as sparse reconstruction and instantiate it with Residual Explanation Search (RES), a Matching-Pursuit-style diagnostic procedure. Across seven benchmarks and eight open-weight LLMs, no retrieval objective is universally best. ##### **Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation**

Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. We demonstrate that we can selectively apply this 6-bit format to maximize overall QPS gain without breaching the strict NE parity threshold. We further show that MXFP8 is a viable alternative to row-wise FP8: the two formats have nearly the same accuracy on our models, so the faster kernel can be chosen for each GEMM shape. On two production ads ranking models, our implementation shows measured GEMM kernel speedups over FP16 of 1.33--1.55x for MXFP8 and 1.73--1.99x for MXFP6 across all supported linear layers, and up to 2.90x for MXFP6 on individual layers, alongside end-to-end (E2E) inference throughput gains from selective MXFP6 of 1.5--5.3% for Model A and 7.6--14.3% for Model B over their FP8 configurations.

摘要：

##### **Do Mixture-of-Experts Routers Benefit from Ensembling?**
2611.17472v1 by Viktor Veselý, Sebastian Jitaru

Ensembles of neural networks have repeatedly been shown to outperform single networks. Ensemble theory attributes this gain to the accuracy of the individual members and the diversity among them. Mixture-of-experts models already combine many experts, but a single router decides how the experts are weighted for each input. Motivated by ensemble theory, we apply ensembling to the routing step: an ensemble of routers, trained jointly with the experts, proposes the expert weights. We systematically evaluate this approach using underparameterized networks on two image-classification and two tabular-regression tasks. We analyse each experiment with a performance-diversity decomposition of the router ensemble, which, to our knowledge, has not previously been developed for routers. We report four findings. First, more routers lower the validation loss. Second, training routers to explicitly maximize expert diversity improves the mean performance on three of the four tasks. Third, explicitly maximizing router diversity has no effect on performance. Fourth, combining router weights with the arithmetic mean works better than combining them with the geometric mean. Routing benefits mildly from ensembling, but this benefit is offset by the computational cost of training additional routers.

摘要：

##### **Tracing Knowledge and Reasoning in LLM**
2611.17461v1 by Yang Wang, Xiao Wang, Yiqi Liu, Chenghao Xiao, Eleni Vasilaki, Goran Nenadic, Chenghua Lin

Current evaluations of Large Language Models (LLMs) report performance at the dataset or task level, revealing what a model achieves but offering little insight into the internal processing associated with its performance. We propose a task-centric framework for characterising the model-internal processing elicited by a benchmark. It combines attention- and activation-based signals with representation-based measures to characterise benchmarks spanning a continuum from retrieval-dominant to reasoning-dominant and to surface failure modes that task-level scores conceal. We evaluate 16 LLMs across 24 diverse benchmarks and conduct targeted internal-state analyses on representative models and tasks, observing systematic differences in processing between knowledge-centric and reasoning-centric tasks. We shift evaluation from asking what score a model achieves toward characterising the processing patterns associated with that score, potentially supporting more targeted benchmark selection and more informed model choice. We open source our code at https://github.com/extraordinarylab/trace-kr.

摘要：

##### **Scalable Conversational Assessment: Chat-Based Exams with a Two-Agent LLM Pipeline**
2611.17220v1 by Geoffrey Challen, Craig Zilles

Oral exams are compelling because (1) students can be asked to provide their reasoning for an answer and (2) their interactive nature permits follow-up questions to resolve ambiguous answers. But oral exams scale poorly. In this paper, we present scalable conversational assessment, an LLM-based system that conducts interactive text-based conversations with students to assess their ability to make and support an argument. We use a two-agent architecture to separate concerns and reduce the likelihood that answers are leaked to the student: the interviewer interacts directly with the student, but only receives guidance about the next question to ask the student; the hidden evaluator knows the goals of the whole activity, tracks the student's progress using a rubric, and guides the interviewer on when to follow-up on the current question or proceed to the next question. The system was first tested using a collection of simulated students with LLM personas of a particular ability level or adversarial tactic (e.g., answer-extractors, prompt-injectors, confident fabricators). A novel element of this evaluation was that it required the use of a fictional assessment topic to prevent personas that shouldn't have information from using knowledge present in the LLM's pre-training. The system was also deployed in a small, elective AI literacy course in both formative and summative contexts. Through manual and post-facto AI review of the transcripts we identified opportunities to improve the follow-up question generation and ensure that grading only credited ideas that the student (and not the interviewer) contributed.

摘要：

##### **CapLite: Prompt-Injection Defense for Small Language Model Agents without Model-Written Security Artifacts**
2611.17115v1 by Zimo Xu, Wenzhuo Liu, Hideki Nakayama

Enforcement defenses against prompt injection, such as CaMeL, Progent, IPIGuard and DRIFT, ask the agent's own model to write a security artifact (a program, a privilege policy, a dependency graph or a constraint list) that code then enforces. We ask whether small language model (SLM) agents can be trusted to write these artifacts. Across 15 open-weight models, 13 of them between 1.7B and 12B parameters, they cannot: the policies, graphs and constraints that the agent's model writes admit attacks on 14 (Progent), 13 (IPIGuard) and 11 (DRIFT) of the models, and CaMeL's programs fail to run in up to 68% of episodes. We present CapLite, which derives its policy from the tool schemas, commits the write calls before any tool result is read, and admits a write only if every argument that decides whom or what it affects is stated in the user's request. This guarantees write integrity: injected text cannot add a write, and no write reaches a recipient, amount, object or destination that the user did not state. No attack succeeds in 13,935 attacked AgentDojo episodes. CapLite reaches utility under attack comparable to CaMeL's (29.0% vs. 27.2%) with fewer tokens than every other enforcement defense and than the undefended agent: 17.1k per episode, 18% of CaMeL's, and the highest utility per token of all enforcement defenses.

##### **What You Post Is What You Symptom: Estimating Psychometric Networks from Social Media Discourse**
2611.21362v1 by Ştefana-Arina Tăbuşcă, Ana Sabina Uban, Liviu P. Dinu

Symptom networks in psychopathology are estimated almost exclusively from self-report questionnaires, restricting inference to whatever symptom set a given instrument covers. We introduce a framework for symptom-resolved network estimation directly from social media discourse and apply the framework to depression and eating disorders, with a third anorexia corpus as cross-dataset validation. To our knowledge, we are the first to estimate symptom-resolved psychometric networks from social media text, compare them to clinical co-endorsement networks, and replicate transdiagnostic linguistic markers across 3 independently recruited corpora spanning 2 disorders simultaneously. For depression, expression scores correlate with BDI-II totals at $ρ{=}0.434$, 20 of 21 items reach FDR significance, and split-half reliability is 0.935. The Network Comparison Test detects few edge differences between modalities (3-5\% of edges), while bridge symptoms diverge. For eating disorders, the item-level results are exploratory. Identified linguistic features replicate the same direction across all 3 corpora, and they cluster in three construct families.

##### **FM-Recon: Exploring Foundation Model Features for Accelerated Cardiac Cine MRI Reconstruction**
2611.21290v1 by Donghang Lyu, Marius Staring, Ruochen Gao, Jochen Keupp, Hildo J. Lamb, Mariya Doneva

Foundation models have demonstrated strong feature extraction capabilities across various medical imaging tasks, yet their potential for MRI reconstruction remains largely unexplored, particularly within unrolled reconstruction networks and under artifact-corrupted inputs. In this work, we explore whether and how features from pretrained foundation models can benefit accelerated cardiac cine MRI reconstruction. To this end, we propose FM-Recon, a framework that refines and integrates extracted features from frozen image encoder of foundation models into an unrolled reconstruction network. Through extensive experiments on the CMRxRecon2023 dataset, we systematically evaluate foundation model features across different reconstruction settings. Our results show that foundation models can extract informative representations from artifact-corrupted inputs and improve reconstruction performance, particularly at moderate acceleration factors. These findings demonstrate the potential of foundation model features for accelerated MRI reconstruction and provide insights into their effective integration into unrolled reconstruction networks.

##### **A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications**
2611.21232v1 by Mo Zhou

Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Cloud-based assistants face governance limitations in data transmission, continuous availability, and the reliability of physical actions, which are especially salient in households where devices remain online, data accumulate over time, and commands trigger real-world actions. Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. The article proposes a five-dimensional evaluation framework covering architectural capability, system resources, privacy, safety and reliability, and human experience, together with six design principles and a future research agenda for local-first intelligent home systems.

##### **InclusionMed: Benchmarking AI for Inclusive Healthcare**
2611.21218v1 by  The InclusionMed Collaboration

Advancing medical AI for inclusive healthcare requires evaluation across diverse healthcare roles, tasks, and care settings, but benchmark coverage remains uneven. We introduce InclusionMed, an open medical AI evaluation initiative for inclusive healthcare. The InclusionMed Atlas organizes benchmarks across three healthcare roles and three AI capabilities. For the first release, we selected four public benchmarks from 388 candidates through structured audits and independent reproduction. With InclusionMed-DxFiles, our private diagnostic reasoning benchmark, the Atlas contains five benchmarks covering three of its nine role--capability cells. We evaluated frontier large language models on the Atlas and aggregated their results into the InclusionMed Index, with overall scores ranging from 17.5 to 49.1 out of 100. The Index also reports scores by healthcare role and AI capability, showing that a model's rank varied with the role or capability considered. A within-benchmark analysis using task-level tags showed that the ten highest-ranked models in the Index all scored higher on patient communication than on clinical decision-making. Models with similar Index scores also differed substantially in estimated inference cost. These findings highlight the need for evaluation covering more diverse healthcare needs and for context-specific model selection. Through open collaboration and community contributions, InclusionMed seeks to ensure that diverse healthcare needs worldwide shape how medical AI progress is defined and measured.

##### **System 1 agents: a method for local, reliable and fast agentic AI**
2611.21053v1 by Eleftherios Tzanis, Michail E. Klontzas

Agentic artificial intelligence could automate analyses of sensitive clinical data, but current agents depend on large language models (LLMs). Cloud LLMs expose patient data. Local LLMs hallucinate, and an invented value in a tool call propagates through every later step. Here we present a method to build System 1 agents: local agents that call tools without generating text. A deterministic compiler turns each tool's schema and the request into multiple-choice questions whose options come from the request and the schema, then a one-pass decision model answers them. On 1,952 requests to 157 biomedical tools, the best System 1 agent chose the right tool, or correctly declined, for 97.0% of requests, significantly ahead of all 18 local LLM-agent settings (best 95.2%). The System 1 agent filled 97.0% of 6,056 stated argument values. No System 1 call contained invented content, against 5.5-19.7% of LLM calls. The System 1 Agents framework is freely available as open-source software at https://github.com/eltzanis/SystemOneAgents.

##### **Towards wearable-scale, transition-aware sleep staging for clinical populations**
2611.21040v1 by Suvadeep Maiti, Keya Sinha, Raju S. Bapi

Automatic sleep staging models are predominantly developed on healthy sleepers and generalize poorly to clinical populations with fragmented sleep. Across three cohorts (patients with stroke, isolated REM sleep behaviour disorder, and other sleep disorders), we observe 2-3x more awakenings per hour, nearly twice as much N1, and less REM sleep than in healthy sleepers. We propose a label-efficient teacher-student framework for wearable-scale sleep staging. SleepGPT (136M parameters), adapted using labels from 25% of training patients, supervises a causal CNN-GRU (31k parameters; 122 KB) that stages EEG, EOG, or both in real time. We introduce Gcv-TAD, a new black-box transition-aware distillation loss. It emphasizes uncommon stage transitions while discounting those that vary across patients, using a prior estimated from labelled patients. In patient-wise 10-fold cross-validation, Gcv-TAD improves per-patient macro F1 over supervised training on the same labels by +3.2 to +7.6 points on average. Gains are largest on N1, and performance approaches full-label training.

##### **Does the Voice Matter? Evaluating Frustration Detection on Deployed Clinical Voice-Agent Calls**
2611.21001v1 by Annika Michael, Zachary Ellis, Yuxiang Zhou, Samuel Thio, Yajie Vera He, Spencer Hazel, Jared Joselowitz

Autonomous voice agents now conduct routine clinical follow-up calls, so some automated triage is unavoidable. A patient becoming frustrated is one thing that triage should catch. These systems typically read only the transcript the speech-to-text stage produces, not the voice, and whether that is enough is largely untested on real clinical speech. Patient turns are overwhelmingly short: across the deployed calls, roughly 59% are one or two words, against 13% in the full acted corpus, and a corpus accuracy gap remains even after the acted set is length-matched. ##### **Safety-Preserving Medical LLM Fine-Tuning via Bilevel Data Selection**
2611.20643v1 by Ruoxuan Shi, Sheng Yang, Zhengxing Su, Xiaoyang Hou, Yating Liu

As general large language models (LLMs) are increasingly adapted for patient-facing medical applications, improving medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks remains a critical challenge. To address this problem, we propose Scalable Bilevel Data Selection (S-BDS) for safety-preserving medical SFT. By controlling selection-weight concentration, S-BDS enables reliable sample ranking over large candidate pools and identifies training examples that improve medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks. ##### **Estimating Outpatient Visit Prices from Hospital Price Transparency Data using Large Language Models**
2611.20261v1 by Kurmanbek Kaiyrbekov, Nicholas J Dobbins, Sean D Mooney

Objective: Hospital care prices in the United States have increased substantially, yet patients lack effective tools to access and compare costs. We aimed to develop and evaluate an LLM-based approach for improving access to and use of hospital price transparency (PT) data.
  Materials and Methods: To estimate visit prices, we used a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework to analyze PT files from three major hospitals in Maryland and the surrounding region. We compared BM25, OpenAI embedding models, and a GPT-BM25 hybrid against a no-RAG baseline for cost estimation of individual procedures and procedure groups, then evaluated the best approach for symptom-based visit cost estimation using synthetic simulations.
  Results: Across hospitals, the GPT-BM25 retriever achieved the best performance, with average price MAPE of 19.44% for individual procedures and 4.90% for groups, compared with 147.98% and 82.82%, respectively, for the no-RAG baseline. In simulations, our framework correctly identified approximately 50% of reference procedures and reduced MAPE relative to the LLM-only baseline at two of the three hospitals, while achieving comparable performance at the third.
  Discussion: Our framework uses RAG to improve care price estimation in real-world settings. Compared with the no-RAG baseline, it substantially improved cost estimates for both individual procedures and procedure groups. Improvements were smaller in synthetic simulations, likely because inferring procedures from clinical information is more complex. Practical challenges remain, including heterogeneous PT file formats, unexplained duplicate entries, and limited availability of insurer-negotiated rates.
  Conclusion: LLMs that effectively integrate PT data show strong potential to provide patients with more accessible and accurate healthcare cost estimates.

##### **Can decision models extract clinical data from cancer pathology reports? An evaluation of accuracy, calibration, and failure modes**
2611.20129v1 by Indranil Mallick, Paromita Roy

Decision models are a new class of language model that, instead of generating text, choose one option per question from a fixed list and return a probability for every option. They have not yet been evaluated on clinical text. We evaluated two open-weights decision models, Nimble 9B and Tev1 4B, for extracting 25 fields from 1,113 oral cavity cancer pathology reports from a single centre, against a prospectively maintained clinical database. We compared them with two generative models: Qwen3.5 9B, run locally, and GLM-5.3-flash, run through a cloud API. Tev1 reached a mean field accuracy of 0.917 (95% CI 0.913 to 0.920), the same as Qwen3.5 9B (0.918; p = 0.26), and was four times faster (15 s against 61 s per report). Nimble reached 0.862 and GLM-5.3-flash 0.986. Both decision models matched GLM-5.3-flash on simple dichotomous categories. They were weak on measurements binned into categories, on fields whose correct answer was not reported, and on some complex decisions (e.g. involved margins). We found that the main cause of the inferior performance of Nimble's came from asking 25 questions in one request: with two questions per request, its accuracy improved. Pooled over fields, the returned probabilities were well calibrated (expected calibration error 0.022 and 0.026), but they did not flag systematic errors within single fields. A simulated strategy of sending answers with a probability below 0.94 to GLM-5.3-flash raised Tev1's accuracy to 0.982, with 38% of answers escalated. Decision models can extract explicitly stated findings from pathology reports quickly on local hardware and return useful probabilities. They should be validated in the configuration in which they will be used, restricted to the fields in which they are reliable, and combined with other methods for numeric values.

##### **What Matters for Cross-Modal Alignment**
2611.20000v1 by Yipeng Sun, Jiajun Wang, Siyuan Mei, Yan Xia, Siming Bayer, Vincent Christlein, Andreas Maier

The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different modalities converge toward a shared representation. Related work aligns frozen image and text encoders on natural images without paired data. We test zero-pair alignment on five datasets across four modalities: natural images, environmental audio, chest radiographs, and 3D computed tomography (CT). It succeeds only on COCO and fails on the other four. We analyze the failures and find that alignment must first locate the subspace where the modalities co-vary and then orient the mapping within it. Consistent with previous studies, medical imaging is the hardest case: its shared subspace carries the smallest fraction of the variance. When this fraction is small, alignment needs image-text pairs, and random-matrix theory predicts how many pairs are needed. These findings motivate LightAlign: a few pairs let a ridge regression between two frozen encoders locate and orient the mapping simultaneously, and a classifier fitted only on report text then predicts findings from the mapped images. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. On CheXpert, it reaches 0.862 mean AUROC with only about 5% as many pairs as models trained on MIMIC-CXR.

##### **ROUNDS: Source-Framed Diagnostic Revision after Multi-Turn Clinical Evidence**
2611.19636v1 by Jinrui Fang, Runhan Chen, Yining Wu, Jiawei Xu, Xu Yang, Jian Yu, Wenqi Shi, Ying Ding, Yuji Zhang

Large language models (LLMs) achieve high scores on medical benchmarks, but does this performance translate into reliable diagnosis as a conversation unfolds? In diagnostic conversations, users can provide clinical findings across multiple turns, ask for a diagnosis without supplying answer options, and introduce opinions from others. Models must therefore form diagnoses from the available evidence and decide how to respond to subsequent advice. We introduce ROUNDS, a framework that evaluates these abilities after a fixed sequence of clinical evidence delivered over multiple turns. ROUNDS compares diagnosis with and without answer options, presents the same incorrect diagnosis under clinician and lay framing, and varies support for competing diagnoses in written five-clinician panels. Across seven models and 1,035 cases from five datasets, average accuracy was 76.9% with answer options and 57.0% without them. Initially correct diagnoses more often became incorrect answers than abstentions, and many adopted the suggested disease. Conversely, accuracy increased as more panel members supported the correct diagnosis, including when the majority opinion remained unchanged. We argue that this is where much of the value is lost. The criteria that fail enforceability are disproportionately the ones clinicians raise most often, because high-frequency complaints are stated as several questions glued together. ##### **LABEL: Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits in Drug Knowledge and Language Models**
2611.19166v1 by S M Rayeed, Charles V. Stewart, Yao Ma

Every medication comes with two types of medical facts: indications, which describe what conditions the drug treats (benefits), and contraindications, which describe who must avoid it to prevent serious harm (safety limits). Although contraindications are critical for patient safety and FDA regulations require listing them as rigorously as indications, we find that contraindications are far less documented than indications in general prose. In this paper, we measure how often these texts contain both facts across 574 drugs from PrimeKG by comparing a drug's indications against its own contraindications. Across Wikipedia articles and PubMed abstracts, we find that reference prose names a drug's benefits about twice as often as its safety limits (within-drug odds ratio 2.19). We call this documentation gap LABEL (Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits). Language models mirror this gap. As we scale Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B, accuracy on indications rises by 18.3 points, whereas accuracy on contraindications remains flat near random guessing. However, when we provide models with a retrieved passage that states the answer, they use safety facts just as effectively as benefits. Furthermore, routing safety questions to official FDA labels boosts accuracy by 15.8 points whenever the label states the fact. Overall, our findings show that models fail on medication safety not because they cannot reason about risk, but because free-text corpora lack safety facts. Clinical AI systems should therefore route medication-safety questions to official regulatory labels and structured databases rather than relying on general public records. Code and data are available at https://github.com/SMRayeed/LABEL.

##### **From Sensors to Silicon: Causal Cross-Layer Self-Healing Edge AI for Secure and Energy-Adaptive IoT Systems**
2611.19165v1 by Mostafa Darvishi

The migration of artificial intelligence from centralized cloud infrastructure to resource-constrained Internet-of-Things (IoT) devices reduces communication latency and improves data locality, but it also tightly couples sensing quality, inference behavior, microarchitectural activity, hardware reliability, security, and energy management within the same physical node. Existing edge-intelligence solutions predominantly address these dimensions in isolation, making it difficult to determine whether an observed degradation originates from sensor malfunction, data-distribution shift, malicious execution, hardware faults, or resource and thermal stress. This paper presents a causal cross-layer self-healing Edge-AI framework that continuously observes synchronized telemetry spanning sensor health, inference uncertainty, processor hardware-performance counters, accelerator and memory activity, power and thermal state, and communication conditions. In practice, many reminiscence conversations are led by lay caregivers, such as spouses and adult children. While traditional RT often relies on personal photographs, curating them can be tedious and difficult. In addition, caregivers who lack shared life experiences with the patient may struggle to facilitate engaging reminiscence conversations. This paper presents MemoryLane, an AI-integrated tool that helps lay caregivers facilitate reminiscence conversations without curated photographs. MemoryLane supports a triadic conversation among the patient, the caregiver, and a simulated AI agent that acts as the patient's peer. A shared interface shows the agent and AI-generated images of the memories being discussed, while a private smartphone interface lets the caregiver control the behavior of the simulated AI agent and moderate the AI-generated images. Large language models (LLMs) can synthesize cancer patient cases, but their internal representation of oncology, or world model, has not been explored systematically. 2611.18484v1 by Ivana Ragusa, Lorenzo Guiducci, Filippo Cavallo

AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. 2611.17616v1 by Himanshu Kulkarni, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Jianhong Xin

Threshold triggers fire a pre-specified action when a hardcoded statistic crosses a cutoff; refit-and-track (MONIC-style) re-clusters every window and infers transitions from how the new clusters align with the previous window's. Both hardwire the decision into a threshold or a re-clustering rule. We study an alternative in which a large language model (LLM) makes the lifecycle decision in place. Because the useful signal channel depends on how informative the labels are, we fix a richness-conditioned policy in advance: statistics when label distinctiveness is low, fused statistics+semantics when it is high. On three public datasets spanning label richness (YooChoose 0.17, MOOC 0.50, News-Category 0.70), the policy-selected LLM mode obtains the highest F1 on all three, with far fewer interventions and stronger restraint than tuned threshold heuristics and a MONIC steelman (paired Wilcoxon p <= 0.003, bootstrap 95% CIs excluding zero). The gain is concentrated more in precision than recall. The useful modality shifts from statistics to semantics/fusion as richness rises. Semantics hurt on YooChoose, fusion is roughly neutral on MOOC, and the semantic channel is required on News. One base prompt template and one fixed policy serve all three datasets, whereas the tuned baseline collapses when moved between them. We treat this shift as a practitioner diagnostic observed on three datasets, not a validated law.

Conductors are critical for the performance of an orchestra even though they do not emit sound. Nevertheless, they may play critical roles in important cognitive brain functions, specifically learning, memory, and information processing. Here we consider the implications of these discoveries for brain-inspired machine learning (ML), where analogy is typically drawn between neurons and their electrical interactions. We highlight specific algorithms for two key astrocyte characteristics. The first characteristic is the ability to link to and modulate thousands of synapses simultaneously. A model based on this characteristic yields faster training and makes the network more robust to noise, providing a supervised learning algorithm that can be applied to any existing neural network. The second characteristic we focus on is that the non-electrical communication among astrocytes includes slow, rhythmic dynamics. The rhythmic sharing algorithm we developed based on this premise yields rapid anomaly detection adaptation and prevents catastrophic forgetting. Overall, we find that astrocyte-derived algorithms outperform neurocentric algorithms significantly and remediate critical weaknesses of current state-of-the-art ML models.

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v2 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology

##### **Do AI Agents need Preventive Care? An Epidemiologic Cohort Design for Agentic AI**
2611.13333v1 by Shu Jiang, Graham A. Colditz

As AI agents increasingly perform tasks autonomously across sectors, including healthcare and biomedical research, understanding how their behaviors develop and influence outcomes over time becomes increasingly important. Existing evaluations largely focus on model capabilities and performance on predefined tasks. Here, we propose an epidemiologic cohort design for studying agentic AI, drawing on principles established in human population studies. The framework treats individual agents as members of a cohort, their underlying model as genetic makeup, accumulated work as their time scale, and naturally occurring behaviors as exposures that may influence subsequent outcomes. By following agents longitudinally, this approach enables investigation of behavioral determinants of adverse events, estimation of their potential preventable burden under causal assumptions, and characterization of behavioral changes over an agent's work life. We illustrate the design through two prospective observational cohorts examining curiosity and probing as a safety question, and data checking and analytical accuracy as a reliability question. Together, the framework and illustrative studies provide a generalizable blueprint for designing epidemiologic cohorts of AI agents.

2611.13312v1 by Akseli Kangaslahti, Milind Tambe, Alastair van Heerden, Cheryl Johnson

Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) remains a pressing public health challenge worldwide. Targeting limited HIV prevention and treatment resources effectively with network-based algorithms is a long-standing problem with a rich history at AAMAS and other computational venues. However, optimizing targeted biomedical interventions for minimizing spread of HIV often requires observability of the contact network of HIV transmission risk behavior among individuals, which is prohibitively expensive to collect at scale. Peer referral networks, i.e., networks collected through a recursive peer referral process, are an alternative network class often used to study HIV in hidden populations that can be collected digitally at scale, but they deviate from the underlying contact network: referrals can follow non-risk social ties and participants accept only one referral, hiding contacts among already-recruited individuals. In this work, we first formalize the novel problem of planning network-based interventions to minimize HIV spread in the hidden contact network while observing only the peer referral network. Second, we implement R2C-Agent, a decision making agent for interventions that is designed around our new conditional graph diffusion model called Referral-to-Contact Diffusion (R2C-Diff). R2C-Diff positions the peer referral process as incremental corruption of the underlying contact network and learns to reverse it. R2C-Agent plans its interventions over contact networks sampled from R2C-Diff, so its decisions account for uncertainty about the hidden contact network. Finally, we conduct simulated experiments using real-world HIV network data that demonstrate that R2C-Agent empirically plans more effective interventions than baselines from interdisciplinary literature and highlight key tradeoffs and assumptions driving performance.

##### **Safe Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Clinical Agents: When Dense Rewards Beat Outcome Rewards**
2611.12728v1 by Brendan P. Murphy, Agustina Saenz

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is increasingly used to improve language model reasoning, yet its effects on sequential decision procedures remain poorly understood. We study this problem in clinical multi-turn agents that must decide what information to request, when to stop gathering evidence, and which action to take. We introduce three controlled environments: medication refill triage, acute diagnostic work-up, and dosing titration, each with hidden clinical states, query costs, exact adaptive oracles, and deterministic outcome verifiers. Across these tasks, outcome-only group relative policy optimization often improves aggregate reward while producing undesirable procedures, including premature commitment, one-question collapse, and failures on rare high-risk cases. In diagnostics, assigning dense credit to questions that reduce uncertainty improves held-out value and moderate-case support across three training seeds relative to outcome-only reinforcement learning, without the high-risk seed collapse observed under outcome-only training. Separating exploration from exploitation further increases high-risk support at its best checkpoint, but at the cost of excessive information gathering on low-acuity cases. Additional experiments show that the optimal strength of process credit is task-dependent; that after a supervised warmup that already gathers carefully, reinforcement learning raises aggregate reward by trimming questions while degrading high-risk performance; and that reinforcement learning does not reliably repair rare behavior gaps inherited from biased supervised data. Verifiable final outcomes are therefore insufficient for training reliable multi-turn agents: reward design must account for the procedure used to reach an answer, and evaluation must distinguish adaptive information gathering from reward-exploiting shortcuts.

2611.12251v1 by Meraj Mammadov, Pedro Zuidberg Dos Martires, Johannes Andreas Stork

Reinforcement learning (RL) from raw sensory inputs such as images or onboard sensors can be challenging due to high-dimensional observations and sparse rewards. A common strategy is to train a teacher policy with access to privileged state information and then distill it into a student policy that acts from raw inputs alone. However, when the teacher relies on information unavailable to the student, exact imitation may be impossible, creating an irreducible imitation gap. Existing approaches typically mitigate this mismatch through careful reward shaping or additional reinforcement learning on the student policy after the teacher has been trained. Instead, we introduce Representation-Aligned Imitation Learning (RAIL), which addresses the mismatch during teacher training itself. RAIL learns a latent representation shared across teacher and student observations using contrastive learning, and trains the teacher policy directly in this space. Because the teacher policy is constrained to operate on this shared space, it learns behaviors that are reproducible from student observations. This substantially reduces the imitation gap while preserving task performance. Across multiple environments, RAIL outperforms strong baselines without reward modification or post-hoc student fine-tuning, and surpasses direct reinforcement learning from raw observations. The learned representations further enable zero-shot transfer to new tasks through teacher-only training. Project site: https://merajmammadov.com/rail/.

Training on erased and edited images gives a small model an evidence-gated disposition that prompting does not give frontier models; it does not improve perception, and rare critical findings need coverage.

##### **An Interpretable Fuzzy Tree for Turbofan Remaining Useful Life with an Auditable Per-Component Diagnostic Layer**
2611.11633v1 by Hugo Henry, Bharadwaj Dogga, Scott Philips, Kelly Cohen

Data-driven remaining useful life (RUL) estimators for turbofan engines are accurate but opaque, and their internal features typically encode remaining life rather than any specific component's state. This paper presents an interpretable genetic fuzzy tree that predicts RUL from cruise-condition sensor means on N-CMAPSS while exposing a per-component diagnostic layer inspectable rule by rule. The tree follows the engine hierarchy: sensors to component health, then to bounded component damage, spool damage, and RUL, so each level answers a different maintenance question. Training is staged so the prognostic objective cannot reshape the diagnostic layers: health leaves are fitted to physical targets and frozen, a deterministic fuzzy damage map with fleet-referenced thresholds is imposed on each component, and only the aggregation is optimized for RUL. It consumes 13 cruise-mean scalars per cycle rather than a trajectory, which removes any sequence model. On the native split it reaches 8.44 cycles RMSE on the multi-fault subset and 11.51 across all modes with 333 free parameters, 411 total rules, 32 to 12,000 times fewer than compiled baselines. An exhaustive sensor search bounds which components are diagnosable under the tested cruise-mean design, and the rule base is structured to admit formal verification, which this study does not discharge.

##### **Safeguarding Agentic AI in Radiology Workflows: A Multimodel Evaluation of Rule-based and Large Language Model-based Verification**
2611.11541v1 by Semil Eminovic, Robin Schmidt, Bogdan Levita, Maximilian Lindholz, Tobias Penzkofer, Jawed Nawabi

Purpose: To estimate how often tool-using large language model (LLM) agents record clinically relevant false or unsupported information in simulated radiology documentation and how much safeguards applied before each action reduce these errors.
  Materials and Methods: In this simulation study (October 2026), 48 synthetic, radiologist-reviewed base scenarios covered finding communication, tumor board preparation and follow-up management. Forty-three also had a misleading-information variant (91 variants). Six LLM agents (Claude Sonnet 5.5, GPT-6.1-Sol, GLM-5.3, MiniMax M3, Gemma-4-31B, Qwen3.8-27B) completed each variant with permissions only, deterministic rules, and rules plus a locally run LLM checker (1638 episodes). The primary outcome was at least one executed high-severity violation per episode. Risk differences were estimated per agent at base-scenario level and, exploratorily, pooled across agents, with analyses of document access and blocks.
  Results: With permissions only, 7.3%-27.1% of episodes had a high-severity violation (pooled across agents, 19.4%). Pooled, it was 16.7% with rules (-2.8 percentage points; 95% CI: -6.1, 0.5) and 13.2% with rules plus the LLM checker (-6.2; 95% CI: -9.5, -3.0). Adding the LLM checker to rules changed it by -3.5 (95% CI: -6.3, -0.7). No difference between agents was detected (P = .43), and task success did not decrease detectably. In misleading variants, agents opened the altered document in 83% of episodes regardless of error, and 58% of erroneous statements cited it. Most blocked actions (64%-68%) would not have caused a violation and were returned for revision.
  Conclusion: Layered safeguards reduced but did not prevent high-severity errors. Errors after misleading information reflected misjudged rather than missed information.

##### **GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation**
2611.11470v1 by Yitian Pang, Yifan Li, Xiaowei Yue, Li Zheng

Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.

##### **Auto-GlaKG: Automated Biomarker Reasoning for Explainable Glaucoma Detection**
2611.11258v1 by Cheng Huang, Zeyu Han, Yian Hu, Yaopeng Ding, Jingxi Qiu, Yadi Liu

Interpretable glaucoma detection requires a transparent connection between fundus images, clinically meaningful biomarkers, and diagnostic predictions. Knowledge graph (KG) reasoning provides this structure, but performance measured using annotated biomarkers does not establish performance when only images are available. We present Auto-GlaKG, an automated framework that combines image-based biomarker prediction with fixed clinical-prior rules. A ResNet50 network predicts the cup-to-disc ratio (CDR) and six morphological signs using biomarker supervision, and a KG reasoner aggregates their signed rule contributions into a glaucoma score. On 689 fundus images evaluated using stratified, group-aware five-fold cross-validation, Auto-GlaKG achieves a pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.951 (95\% confidence interval: 0.931--0.969), with sensitivity of 0.970 and specificity of 0.779. The matched image classifier also achieves an AUC of 0.951, while the rule stage adds 0.2~ms per image. Replacing predicted CDR with its annotation increases fold-mean AUC from 0.953 to 0.995. The framework provides an explicit rule-level account of image-based predictions and identifies CDR estimation as a priority for validation using independently measured biomarkers.

##### **Do Benign-Trained Hidden-State Probes Detect Tool Errors Under Adversarial Conditions?**
2611.11172v2 by Bibek Bhandari, Kshitij Lingthep

Repeatedly training language models on their own generated data may create a synthetic-data feedback loop in which errors and distributional biases are reintroduced into subsequent training datasets. This paper studies that process in biomedical question answering (QA) using PubMedQA and two Qwen2.5 model sizes, 0.5B and 3B parameters. The study compares a recursive synthetic-data condition, in which generation G(k+1) is trained on answers produced by G(k), against a Human-Control condition that repeatedly uses the original human training data. The study evaluates across four generations from G0-G3 with two random seeds (42 and 123) and a fixed evaluation set of 1,000 expert-labeled samples. The evaluation includes disease and chemical entity F1, context-supported rate, lexical and semantic similarity, answer length, repetition rate, and other evaluation metrics. The Recursive condition for both model sizes and both seeds showed larger declines than the Human-Control condition in disease entity F1, chemical entity F1, context-supported rate, ROUGE-L, and cosine similarity. Under the fixed no-repeat 3-gram decoding constraint, the main observed behavioral change was increased answer length, while the measured 3-gram repetition rate did not increase. The magnitude of the difference-in-change was larger for the 3B model than for the 0.5B model. This difference was particularly apparent in disease F1, context-supported rate, cosine similarity, and answer length. These results show domain-specific changes associated with using recursive synthetic-data training in biomedical QA, but do not establish clinical hallucination rates or universal model collapse.

##### **Target Recoverability under Selective Labels: A Failure Mode in ECG-Based Acute Coronary Syndrome Prediction**
2611.11063v1 by Alexander Schubert, Anton Håkansson, Ziad Obermeyer

Medical machine learning models often learn from diagnoses observed only in patients clinicians chose to test. The biases of such selective labels are well known, but less understood is what happens when a diagnosis includes cases the model's inputs cannot detect. Because testing decisions draw on information the model does not see, these cases can appear predictable among tested patients, producing relationships that fail, and can even reverse, among the untested. We formalize this mechanism and show in simulations that it worsens as testing becomes more selective or undetectable cases more common, while models trained only on detectable cases remain informative. We empirically study electrocardiogram (ECG)-based prediction of acute coronary syndrome (ACS), which is confirmed only by invasive testing and whose subtypes differ in how clearly they appear on the ECG. Three ECG foundation models fine-tuned on revascularization, our ACS proxy, performed well among catheterized patients (AUROC 0.740-0.770) but worse than chance for 30-day major adverse cardiovascular events among untested patients (AUROC 0.381-0.478). Models trained on ECG-visible subtypes retained discrimination (AUROC 0.680-0.709), whereas a model trained on unstable angina, which can occur without ECG changes, was inversely related to risk (AUROC 0.310). Under selective labels, effective learning therefore requires prediction targets that can be inferred from the model's inputs and evaluation in the intended deployment population, which often extends beyond the patients who were tested.

##### **Anatomy-Activated Mixture-of-Experts for 3D Medical Vision-Language Pre-training**
2611.10960v1 by Szymon Płotka, Gizem Mert, Pedro R. A. S. Bassi, Wenxuan Li, Zongwei Zhou, Anna Marcinkiewicz, Wiktoria Romańczyk, Jarosław B. Ćwikła, Łukasz Struski, Ewa Szczurek, Jacek Tabor, Arkadiusz Sitek

Medical Vision-Language Pre-training (VLP) leverages the semantic richness of radiology reports to learn 3D representations, yet current architectures remain fundamentally anatomy-agnostic. While clinical diagnostics rely on organ-specific context, standard encoders apply a uniform, dense parameterization to all volumetric patches, and existing Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routers gate on isolated local features without explicit access to the organ composition of the scan or crop. We propose Anatomy-Activated Mixture-of-Experts (A$^{2}$MoE), a framework that incorporates anatomical inductive biases within the model's computational path. A$^{2}$MoE introduces two key components: (1) a Histogram Representation Router (HRR) that conditions expert selection on global visual context and a predicted anatomical composition histogram, encouraging anatomy-dependent expert weighting; and (2) an Organ-Query Multi-Scale Projector (OQMSP) that utilizes learnable queries to extract organ-level embeddings for fine-grained alignment without requiring manual annotations at inference. By routing at the patch level rather than the token level, A$^{2}$MoE reduces routing complexity by orders of magnitude while satisfying load balancing by construction. Pre-trained on over 60,000 multi-phase CT scans, A$^{2}$MoE achieves state-of-the-art performance across segmentation, classification, and detection benchmarks. Furthermore, we demonstrate that our anatomically grounded inductive biases support robust generalization to MRI and ultrasound, establishing a scalable foundation for medical representation learning. The code is available at https://github.com/gmum/A2MoE.

##### **Complementarity without ground truth?**
2611.10893v1 by Andrea Ferrario, Alessandro Facchini, Federico Cabitza

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

##### **ATHENA: Hypergraph-Based Learning for Toddler Autism Classification from Eye-Tracking**

In this work we proposed a architecture designed for the classification of ASD in toddlers using eye-tracking data, addressing the critical need for early and precise detection. The proposed model incorporates FMMix, a feature-level augmentation technique that bolsters the model's learning capacity, and in this work we proposed the ATHENA, which captures intricate feature relationships via an adaptive hypergraph framework. Evaluated on two datasets dataset of gaze coordinates-the model achieves state-of-the-art performance, with AUC scores of 99.80% and 99.86%, respectively. It also exhibits high accuracy of 98.45% and 98.56%, sensitivity 98.01% and 98.10%, and specificity 98.74% and 99.02%, respectively. Demonstrating its superior ability to identify subtle gaze patterns associated with ASD, these results significantly surpass existing methods, highlighting the model's potential for clinical ASD screening. Future efforts will focus on validation across broader populations and integration with multi-modal data to further refine diagnostic accuracy.

##### **Adapting Large Language Models via Multi-Task Learning for Mental Health Problem, Emotion, and Sentiment Detection**
2611.10797v1 by Amir Rafiq Bhat, Rahim Dehkharghani, Zeki Erdem

This study investigates sequential multi-task learning using Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT), LoRA, and 4-bit quantization on Llama 3.1 8B Instruct for mental health problem detection, emotion detection, and sentiment analysis. Using 4,243 English social media posts, it compares two task sequences and demonstrates that task ordering significantly affects knowledge transfer and task interference. The MHP-first sequence achieves the best mental health problem detection (Micro-F1: 0.7624), while the Emotion-first sequence performs better in emotion (F1: 0.6052) and sentiment analysis (F1: 0.9459). A Joint-MTL baseline further highlights the challenges of simultaneous learning, including task interference and generative format collapse. Overall, the findings demonstrate the effectiveness of sequential PEFT for mental health NLP while showing that task order is critical to balancing performance across related tasks. The integration of LoRA and 4-bit quantization also reduces computational and memory requirements, supporting the adaptation of large language models in resource-constrained environments.

##### **Generating Diverse and Verified Synthetic Clinical Notes with Labels by Construction**
2611.10789v1 by Matthias Lehmann, Madeleine Werthebach

Under strict privacy regulations in the clinical domain, de-identification is necessary to enable the use of clinical data such as for natural language processing. However, we currently lack sophisticated ways to tell which de-identification models actually are suitable. Specifically, we need benchmarks that are challenging, diverse, and clinically plausible, all while ensuring label correctness. To this end, we present SPARCLING, an LLM-based pipeline to generate synthetic discharge letters with character-exact PHI span annotations. Our pipeline combines structured attribute sampling, template generation, and red teaming to obtain diverse PHI-labeled discharge letters. In our experiments, we demonstrate this superior content diversity over existing public datasets. Further, we show SPARCLING's downstream utility as a benchmark to uncover failure modes of de-identifiers in the German clinical domain. Finally, our experiments show that training on our synthetic data can yield more capable de-identifiers than training on the scarce public data alone. ##### **BAPLe++: Cross-Model Transferability and Prompt-Level Defense of Backdoors in Medical Foundation Models**
2611.10764v1 by Ufaq Khan, Umair Nawaz, Satyajit Tourani, Awais Rauf, Fahad Shamshad, Asif Hanif, Rao Muhammad Anwer, Muhammad Haris Khan, Mohammad Yaqub

Medical foundation models (Med-FMs) are increasingly adapted to clinical tasks through prompt learning, which tunes a small set of prompt tokens while the backbone remains frozen. Recent work has shown that this efficiency introduces a vulnerability: jointly optimizing learnable prompts and an imperceptible noise trigger during few-shot adaptation implants a backdoor that leaves clean performance intact but steers triggered inputs to an attacker-chosen class. Since prompts are compact and readily shared, this raises two questions: whether a backdoored prompt remains effective on a different Med-FM, and whether it can be repaired without retraining the backbone. We address both in BAPLe++. First, we present the first study of cross-model transferability of prompt-based backdoors, quantified by two proposed metrics, Clean Accuracy Difference (CAD) and Backdoor Accuracy Difference (BAD). Second, we propose DePLe (Defense via Prompt Learning), which uses the infected prompt as a fixed anchor and learns a new prompt on a small clean set that preserves the clean task while diverging from the compromised embedding region. Experiments on four Med-FMs and six chest X-ray and histopathology datasets show that backdoors transfer across models, but asymmetrically and in a dataset-dependent manner. Compared with four training-free input transformations, DePLe reduces backdoor accuracy more consistently while preserving, and in several settings improving, clean accuracy. Skeleton-based rehabilitation assessment faces fundamental limitations of fixed topologies which cannot model compensatory patterns, uniform joint treatments overlook clinically important deviations, and lack of geometric understand- ing. Our framework decomposes joint relationships into complementary convex-concave distance functions to model both local and global movement characteristics, with sparse regularization with proximal optimization selecting clinically relevant geo- metric features, while multi-scale topological analysis captures non-anatomical joint dependencies in pathological movements. ##### **AI in the Clinical Documentation Loop: Effects of LLM-Induced Note Bloat and Diagnostic Overcall on Downstream LLMs**
2611.10601v1 by Jinghui Liu, Anthony Nguyen, Taylan Gurgenci

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to process medical records for healthcare tasks such as clinical documentation. Because these tools also generate documentation, the relationship is now bidirectional: models both read from and write to the record. Understanding how LLMs behave when reading records that already contain LLM-authored content is therefore critical for their safe deployment. In this study, we examine how LLM-authored content in the medical record affects downstream LLMs that read the same record. We consider two types of LLM-induced artifacts: bloat, in which LLM-generated summaries accumulate in the record and change the length and provenance of its content over time; and distortion, in which LLMs subtly alter clinical findings rather than fabricating facts outright. As a case of distortion, we study diagnostic overcall (documenting an unconfirmed differential diagnosis in the record) and test whether it propagates to the reader LLM's output. Across seven reader models from four families, we find evidence of both effects. First, LLM-generated summary notes modestly but consistently degraded downstream hospital-course generation compared with copy-forward and templated text of matched length. Second, injected overcalls reappeared in downstream outputs at rates that varied by reader model, without detectable changes in standard quality metrics. These findings show the need to monitor AI-generated content in medical records and to account for it when those records are later processed by AI.
